# ∃R ⊆ CH 

# Alex Meiburg 29 September 2026 

The content in this paper is written essentially entirely by ChatGPT, and was completed after a series of conversations ending on September 29th 2026. A group of researchers has been working to digest the proof, and while the most essential arguments appear correct, we are endeavoring to give this result the treatment it deserves and a proper exposition and development to benefit of the community. However, on October 6th, OpenAI released a very similar proof that the ∃R ⊆ CH . Their proof is dated as a work done on October 4th, thus, roughly a week after our co-developing of this manuscript with ChatGPT was completed. The headline result is the same: the existential theory of the reals, indeed with any constant level of alternating existential-universal quantifiers, is contained in the Counting Hierarchy. This manuscript places ∃R at a significantly lower level of the hierarchy, specifically BPP C3P ⊆ C4P, while OpenAI’s result places it under the weaker bound ∃R ⊆ C26 P. This work also includes new results about the difficulty of 

PosSLP , the Square-Root-Sum problem, SDPs, and others, that are absent from their version. While we work to improve our exposition of this proof, the current version has been uploaded as a service to the community to compare the different proof techniques. Alex Meiburg is listing himself as an author on this version by the standard that every paper needs an author. While he takes responsibility that the proofs appear to be correct, he has not played a nontrivial role in developing them, and believes the human value will be in good exposition and canonicalization of the results. 

Abstract 

The existential theory of the reals asks whether polynomial constraints with integer coefficients have a real solution. We give a proof placing this problem in the counting hierarchy. The first argument is intended to expose the essential steps: a coercive minimum reduces feasibility to a finite critical algebra; residue formulas give its multiplication traces; and a short collection of derivative signs selects a real root. Exact formal power series and univariate resultants complete the calculation. This proof permits singular critical fibers and does not require a multivariate resultant algorithm. A separate analysis lowers the bound to ∃R ⊆ BPP C3P ⊆ C4P. It uses a common formal homotopy, ordered labels for real roots, and randomized integer-sign reconstruction. For each fixed w, sentences with w alternating real quantifier blocks lie in C9w+17 P. We then treat exact semidefinite feasibility, PosSLP , square-root sum, geometric real counting, Euler characteristic, and complex feasibility in separate applications. The corresponding bounds include BPP C2P for general SDP, BPP PP ∩ PNP PP 

for PosSLP and square-root sum, and FP C4P for total geometric real counting. Longer arithmetic proofs, conditional certificate routes, and limitations of other approaches are collected in the supplementary sections.

# 1 Introduction 

The existential theory of the reals, or ETR, is the decision problem for finite systems of polynomial equations and inequalities over R. Its input is a finite binary string; its witnesses are real tuples. The associated class ∃R consists of the languages polynomial-time many-one reducible to this problem. It captures many geometric existence problems, including the art gallery problem [7]. The usual general bounds are 

NP ⊆ ∃ R ⊆ PSPACE [8] .

An obstacle to a smaller upper bound is that a solution can require very large coordinates and high algebraic degree. A bound on the size of a coordinate is not a short description of that coordinate. The counting hierarchy is defined by 

C0P = P, Ch+1 P = PP ChP, CH = ⋃

> h≥0

ChP.

2It can process some exponentially large arithmetic objects without writing them down. Allender, Bürgisser, Kjeldgaard-Pedersen, and Miltersen used this principle for integer straight-line programs [11]. Andrews, Garg, and Schost recently placed Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz, the corresponding complex feasibility problem, in CH 

[10]. Over the reals there is another task: identify the solutions that are real and satisfy the required order conditions. The sign of a carefully chosen norm will carry this information. The paper proceeds in four stages. Section 2 proves ∃R ⊆ CH without optimizing the level. It gives the algebra and the real-root selection argument explicitly, starting from standard integer arithmetic and the known bit-access theorem for straight-line programs. Section 3 changes the implementation and the selector to obtain the fourth-level bound. Section 4 treats fixed real quantifier blocks, including an explicit linear level bound. Section 5 gives the other complexity consequences, with the input model and promise stated for each problem. We distinguish a randomized polynomial-time machine with a PP oracle, denoted BPP PP , from an outer probability operator such as BP · PP . We also do not identify PH PP with PPP . Oracle order matters in the arguments below. Function problems, such as exact point counting, are stated in function classes rather than decision classes. 

# 2 The basic containment: from critical points to a real-root test 

Theorem 1. The existential theory of the reals belongs to the counting hierarchy: ∃R ⊆ CH .

There are two distinct issues in the proof. We first replace an arbitrary real solution set by a finite algebra whose real points answer the original question. We then compute with that algebra through traces and univariate polynomials. No step assumes a short rational witness for the original instance. 

# 2.1 What it means to compute an exponential object 

Write L for the original input length. Throughout this section, degrees, dimensions, and integer bit lengths are bounded by 2 poly( L). Their indices therefore have polynomially many bits. An integer array is CH-accessible 

if the sign and a specified bit of a specified entry can be decided uniformly in one fixed level of CH . The level is fixed for the construction; it does not grow with an index or the number of variables. We use two standard computational facts. First, a specified bit of the integer output of a polynomial-size arithmetic straight-line program is in CH [11]. We call this the BitSLP theorem. Second, division and iterated integer multiplication have suitably uniform TC 0 circuits [1]; addition, comparison, and parallel selection do as well. Here TC 0 means polynomial-size, constant-depth Boolean circuits with unbounded majority gates. 

Lemma 1. A fixed-depth, uniformly generated threshold computation on exponentially many exponentially long integers preserves CH-accessibility. This applies to a fixed number of compositions of the preceding integer operations and to dense polynomial addition, multiplication, differentiation, and bounded composition, provided their degrees and coefficient bit bounds remain singly exponential. Proof. Every gate and wire has a polynomial-length address. At a majority gate, a polynomial-time counting machine guesses an input-wire address and queries the predicate for that wire. Thus one threshold layer costs at most one further PP -oracle level. There are only constantly many layers. For polynomial arithmetic, choose an integer base larger than twice a bound on every coefficient magnitude and evaluate at that base. Iterated integer multiplication computes a product of polynomials; parallel balanced-digit extraction recovers its signed coefficients. The packed integer has polynomial length in the expanded degree and coefficient bounds. Derivatives use factorial-ratio weights, and composition uses parallel powers and sums. Compatible uniformity is essential: merely having an exponential-size circuit would not prove the assertion. We will also use the following direct consequence of BitSLP. A specified coefficient of a polynomial-size arithmetic circuit has CH-accessible bits. Split the circuit into a difference of two circuits with nonnegative coefficients. For each part, Kronecker substitution xi 7 → B(D+1) i−1

, with sufficiently large power-of-two B,encodes coefficients as nonoverlapping blocks. The substituted integers and exponents have short arithmetic-circuit descriptions. BitSLP reads the requested block, and Lemma 1 handles the signed subtraction. An SLP with s gates has degree at most 2 s and coefficient bit bound 2 poly( s,L ), so the required addresses remain short.

# 2.6 Select a real root using only a short list of signs 

A norm multiplies values over all complex points. Nonreal points pair with their conjugates and contribute positively. Negative factors at real points are therefore detectable, but only modulo two. We arrange that some short choice selects exactly one qualifying real root. 

Lemma 5. Let b, e, v be CH-accessible integer univariate polynomials of singly exponential degree and bit height, with b̸ = 0 . Suppose every real root satisfying e = 0 , v > 0 is simple in b. Existence of such a root is decidable in a fixed level of CH .

6Proof. Normalize the leading coefficient of b to be positive, and write D = deg b. A constant polynomial has no roots, so assume D ≥ 1. Thom’s lemma says that the vectors (sgn b′(α), . . . , sgn b(D)(α)) distinguish its distinct real roots. In a nonempty collection of qualifying roots, choose a coordinate that varies and retain a smallest nonempty sign class. This removes at least half the roots. Repeating at most 

⌈log 2 D⌉ times isolates one qualifying root. Thus a list of O(log D) derivative indices and trits suffices; its binary length is polynomial in L.To turn a guessed list into a polynomial, bound all nonzero test values at roots of b between 2 −K and 2 K ,with singly exponential K. Cauchy’s bound gives the upper estimate. For the lower estimate, the resultant of a primitive irreducible factor of b with a test polynomial is a nonzero integer; all its other factors have the upper bound. This argument uses a factor only to prove the bound, and does not compute a factorization. There is an explicit rational approximation R = A/B to the ternary sign on these values, with B > 0 on 

R and R(0) = 0. For clarity, take an even integer m and define

A nonselected real root contributes positively with any multiplicity. A selected root is simple and contributes one negative factor. The coefficient sign is consequently ( −1) #matches .Corollary 1 computes this polynomial’s coefficients in CH; evaluation and interpolation, or one larger packed evaluation, handles its parameter u. Parallel zero tests locate its first nonzero coefficient. All degrees and heights are still singly exponential and all constructions have fixed arithmetic depth. Accept a guessed list when that coefficient is negative. Soundness requires only a nonzero odd number of matches. Completeness follows from the existence of a list isolating one root. The final polynomial-length guess costs one further level, since NP Ck P ⊆ Ck+1 P.

All derivatives and sums above are formed in parallel using their coefficient formulas, so the number of strata does not become the depth of the computation. By (1) and (4), acceptance is equivalent to the original real feasibility question. This proves Theorem 1. The proof has used only a fixed number of CH arithmetic stages above BitSLP. It has not assigned them a small numerical depth. In particular, the norm or the packed characteristic polynomial is not asserted to have a polynomial-size integer SLP. The next section obtains a smaller bound by a different implementation of the finite-algebra calculation. 

# 3 Lowering the bound to the fourth level 

Theorem 2. 

∃R ⊆ BPP C3P ⊆ C4P.

The preceding proof deliberately hides the depth of integer arithmetic. Unhiding it is not enough to obtain this bound. We make three changes. First, a special critical-point construction gives simple points and distinct critical values. Second, these values receive short, distinct integer labels, so a random affine hash can isolate a qualifying value. Third, modular norm evaluation and integer-sign reconstruction are combined before an unnecessary oracle layer is introduced. We explain the interfaces here; the complete arithmetic proofs are in Sections G, I, and J.5 of the supplementary material. 

# 3.1 A critical algebra with distinct values 

Use the bounded version of ETR in which variables lie in [1 /2, 2] and constraints have the forms x = 1, 

x + y = z, and xy = 1 [7]. Enforce the interval constraints with 2x − 1 − 2u2 = 0 , 2 − x − v2 = 0 .

The sum F of the squares of all these equations is a coercive quartic. Its zeros have all coordinates bounded by two. Write m for the number of coordinates. An effective minimum bound supplies a short binary integer 

E0 such that min F = 0 or min F ≥ 2−E0 .

The parameters can be chosen with E0 ≥ 2m. Set 

C0 = 91(2 m − 1) , A0 = C0 + 1 , Q0 = A02E0 , T = 3 A0, wi = 2 i−1,

This polynomial has five distinct roots over F64 . Hensel lifting gives D simple characteristic-zero points. Moreover, U 6 + 3( U + 1) 2 ≡ U + 1 (mod 2 , p (U )). Lemma 9 gives the full argument and height bounds. This small-characteristic construction replaces the multiplicity strata of the basic proof. The polynomial 

q(Z) = Norm( Z − J) is now squarefree and its real roots correspond exactly to the real critical points. 

# 3.2 Integer-sign reconstruction as an interface 

An exponentially large integer cannot be signed by selecting a single short modulus. We use its residues simultaneously. The following is the form of the arithmetic interface needed below. 

Lemma 6. Let Z ∈ Z have a known singly exponential bit bound. Suppose that for every positive, polynomial-bit modulus a, an integer representative of Z mod a is given by a raw GapP A expression, with polynomial-length branch addresses. Then the randomized sign algorithm uses BPP PP A

. The same assertion holds for the particular representatives below after the indicated functional counting absorption. 

Here “raw” means that the outer signed sum remains a counting expression. Calling an oracle to retrieve and then sum its canonical residues can cost an extra level. The hypothesis also concerns all short moduli, including prime powers. Residues only modulo large primes do not suffice for this interface. For orientation, the underlying identity is 

> N

∑

> i=0

)

tN (1 − t)N dt = (N !) 2

(2 N + 1)! > 0. (6) Replace the binomial coefficients and Z by integer representatives modulo N + 1 + i. The resulting rational sum is an integer plus δ = CN Z, where CN is the positive quantity in (6). Choosing N above the bit bound makes |δ| tiny, with a known lower bound when it is nonzero. Dyadic multiplication of this phase eventually makes its sign visible before wrapping around the unit circle. A short-seed, random-access generator and a robust search over scales locate such a phase in randomized polynomial time. All weighted sums stay inside the PP A oracle. Section I proves the generator, phase test, and search; in particular it does not assume that independently generated exponential-length randomness is available. There is also a deterministic first-event search. For the integer SLP representatives, its oracle is NP PP ,giving PNP PP 

. We return to this in the applications. 

# 3.3 Ordered labels instead of full derivative sign vectors 

The basic proof isolates a root using a short part of its Thom vector. For the smaller bound we first assign an integer label to each real critical value. Let α1, . . . , α D be all the roots of q, including the nonreal roots. The rational normalizer of Section J.5 is a short real-coefficient circuit R = n/d . Its denominator is positive on R, it vanishes at zero, and it preserves the sign of every nonzero real argument. These real-line properties hold for every seed. With high probability, simultaneously for all nonzero differences αi − αj , its value is very close to one of 1 , −1. Its additional ordering property is essential: along each horizontal line, the limiting half-plane signs on the good inputs are nondecreasing from left to right (Lemma 19). No global preservation of the original complex half-plane is asserted. Denote these limiting signs by σij , with σii = 0. For a real root put 

Si =

9Conjugate roots contribute paired signs; hence ri is an integer between zero and D − 1. As the real root moves to the right, every column is nondecreasing, and the two columns belonging to a pair of real roots force a strict increase. The labels ri are therefore distinct on the real roots. We need not list the D summands. A first-order norm computes them all through the identity 

D0(u) + ϵN 0(u) = Norm (d(u − J) + ϵn (u − J)) (mod ϵ2). (7) Where D0(u)̸ = 0, its logarithmic coefficient is

Consequently X(u) = ( N0(u) + ( D − 1) D0(u)) /(2 D0(u)) lies within a small constant of ri at each real root. Short polynomial circuits read the bits of this nearby integer: the sign of sin( π(X + 1 /2) /2b) gives the corresponding bit, and range reduction, Taylor approximation, and repeated squaring supply the required polynomial approximation. The distance from the sign boundary is at least an inverse exponential. The proof in Section J.5 includes the approximation error and positive denominator clearing. Now hash the ℓ = ⌈log 2 D⌉-bit labels by a random affine map h : Fℓ 

> 2

→ Fk 

> 2

. A suitable output length isolates exactly one qualifying label with constant probability. Guessing that length randomly costs only an inverse polynomial factor. The polynomial selector in (51) combines the hash equations with the condition 

J < T . Its sign is negative at a matching qualifying real root and positive at every other real root. Even an unsuccessful normalization seed cannot make a nonqualifying real root negative. After a sufficiently large positive scaling and a shift by one, the selector is nonzero at every complex root. The norm is then negative exactly when an odd number of qualifying real roots were selected: conjugate pairs contribute positive factors. 

# 3.4 Why the arithmetic costs three oracle levels 

Both the inner norm (7) and the final selector norm are evaluated with the same formal homotopy. Over a short finite ring, start from a split reference system with unit Jacobian and continue each branch formally. At a fixed evaluation node a polynomial number of Newton updates suffices, because each update doubles the available order. Degree bounds permit Fourier coefficient extraction without aliasing. Products and sums have exponentially many factors but polynomial-length addresses. There is no sequential walk through all critical points. For arbitrary prime powers the calculation takes place in a short unramified Galois ring. Nonunits in a product are handled by their valuations; unit products and first-order product jets have counting representatives (Lemmas 14 and 18, and the jet lemma in Section J.5). This supplies the all-moduli hypothesis of Lemma 6. The optimized accounting is as follows. The output of a function computation at a node has polynomially many bits; the large final integer is always represented by residues. Operation Complexity at a short modulus One formal branch and a local circuit value P

Inner norm jet, hence rank and hash factors FP PP 

Outer product over branches FP C2P

Final Fourier sum, kept as a signed sum GapP C2P

Randomized sign reconstruction BPP C3P

The last Fourier extraction is merged with the weighted count in sign reconstruction. It is not first turned into a new canonical residue oracle. Polynomially many isolation trials and error amplification preserve 

BPP C3P. Since BPP A ⊆ PP A relativizes, the second containment in Theorem 2 follows. The intermediate bounds C1023 P, C7P, C6P, and C5P record different implementations and isolation methods. They are explained in supplementary Section J. They are not successive consequences of a general collapse of the counting hierarchy. 

10 4 A linear bound for the real polynomial hierarchy 

Write Σ wR for the discrete class defined by polynomial-size integer polynomial formulas with w alternating blocks of real quantifiers, beginning existentially, and write Π wR for the universal counterpart. The number of variables in a block is not bounded. We use the constant-free, discrete real polynomial hierarchy terminology of [29]; binary rational constants cause no change. In particular Σ 1R = ∃R.

Theorem 3. For every fixed positive integer w,

ΣwR ∪ ΠwR ⊆ C9w+17 P.

Consequently the union of the fixed levels of the discrete real polynomial hierarchy is contained in CH .

The coefficient of w is an accounting bound, not an optimality claim. For w = 1, Theorem 2 gives the much better level four. The quantifier fixed is part of the theorem: the degree of the running-time polynomial may depend on w. This does not place sentences of input-dependent quantifier depth in one constant level of 

CH .

# 4.1 Why a Boolean ETR oracle is insufficient 

After choosing a sample for an outer block, the coefficients seen by an inner block can be algebraic numbers. They need not have short binary representations. Moreover, a later sign test must refer to the same 

earlier sample, rather than a separately guessed root with convenient signs. We therefore need a uniform representation of sample tuples, together with their validity and sign predicates. The block-elimination construction of Basu, Pollack, and Roy [28, Algorithms 14.1–14.2] supplies the geometric starting point. We retain its coverage of connected sign components and replace its sequential arithmetic by parallel resultant calculations. Section O.10 gives the construction in detail; we summarize the invariant and then give the numerical accounting. Each block supplies exponentially indexed univariate representations of candidate samples. A represen-tation consists of a defining polynomial q, a real root α, and rational coordinates gi(α)/g 0(α). Its validity includes g0(α)̸ = 0, tested at that root. Bounds on degrees, coefficient heights, and family sizes remain singly exponential for fixed w. The signs of bounded polynomial expressions in earlier samples are available uniformly; merely knowing the signs of the individual coefficients would not suffice. There are three points at which coherence could otherwise fail. First, we keep degree and gcd metadata for every specialization. These data prevent roots from splitting, colliding, or acquiring new zeros of a guard inside one connected parameter cell. Thus the real root branches and the required signs continue on that cell. Equality of sign vectors on disconnected parameter sets does not justify such a conclusion. Second, roots have short, coherent addresses. Hash the full Thom vector, implement bucket parity by a resultant, and count the odd buckets. If their sizes are nt and there are r real roots, 

r − #{t : nt odd } = ∑

> t

(nt − (nt mod 2) ) ≥ 0.

The hash family contains an injective member. Hence every hash maximizing the number of odd buckets is injective. A maximizing hash and an occupied bucket name one root, and subsequent tests reuse that address. This gives a sign oracle for an entire indexed family without guessing its exponential sign profile. Third, all formal infinitesimals are specialized once , for the whole guarded sample tree. A complete candidate tuple uses only a number of algebraic root variables depending on w. Fixed-dimensional elimination bounds all polynomials governing the truth of the finite representation formula. Choose 

ϵi = η(M +1) i−1

, η = 2 −B ,

with M, B above these effective degree and height bounds. Leading infinitesimal monomials then dominate simultaneously. The finite tuple and the formal tuple lie in the same relevant cell and give the same tree truth. Independent choices for different branches would not prove this. The constants have short arithmetic circuits despite their very small values. 

11 Finally, recode a complete candidate tuple into a single finite algebra. Parent–child consistency becomes equality of coordinate prefixes of complete tuples. A comparison of two tuples uses two root addresses, rather than a new nested tower at every comparison. Existential blocks quantify over valid short addresses; universal blocks use validity as the antecedent of an implication. Duplicate representations are harmless. This reduces the original real sentence to fixed Boolean alternation over coherent CH predicates. 

# 4.2 The arithmetic primitives and the level ledger 

The general resultant primitive has the following useful refinement. If the coefficients of its input forms are available at level h, its output coefficient residues are available in FP Ch+3 P. If instead their exact homogeneous values and the needed first derivatives at short nodes are already available in FP Cr P, the output uses only 

FP Cmax( r, 1)+2 P. The first remaining layer computes the flattened Poisson product, and the second extracts coefficients. The evaluator includes points at infinity: it may not divide by a zero homogenizing coordinate. For restricted elimination with degree drops, use the padded resultant Res D,E 

(f + t, g + λt + V X E ), D ≥ deg f, E > deg g even . (8) If the specialized degree of f is d > 0, its first nonzero V -slice occurs at D − d. Up to a nonzero leading-coefficient factor independent of t, that slice is the ordinary deformed resultant Res( f + t, g + λt ). Its t-order is deg gcd( f, g ): at a common root the local order is the minimum of the two multiplicities, and the formal 

λ prevents cancellation. A nonzero constant f = a gives ( a + t)E V D; the identically zero case is detected separately. Thus one coefficient family retains the degree and gcd data, without a sign-dependent truncation inside the arithmetic algorithm. A further saving comes from evaluating derivative-coordinate polynomials in the same Fourier sum that constructs them. If q(T ) = ∑Di=0 qiT i, the homogeneous value of its μ-th derivative at ( B, A ) is

This saving applies to these represented derivative families, not to arbitrary coefficient arrays. Here is the complete conservative ledger. # 5 Other problems and complexity consequences 

The same arithmetic interfaces have different costs in different applications. A local sign condition uses one norm; the ordered labels used for full real feasibility require a norm inside another norm. This distinction accounts for several of the smaller bounds below. All inputs are finite binary descriptions. The bounds in this section are consequences of the draft’s proofs, with the same status as its headline theorem. 

# 5.1 Semidefinite feasibility 

For explicitly sized symmetric rational matrices, let 

A(x) = A0 +

> n

∑

> i=1

xiAi.

General SDP feasibility asks whether A(x) ⪰ 0 for some real x; strict SDP feasibility asks whether A(x) ≻ 0. These are exact decision questions, with no numerical gap promise. 

Theorem 4. General and strict rational SDP feasibility belong to BPP C2P ⊆ C3P. More generally, the same bound holds for feasibility of a quantifier-free semialgebraic set promised to be closed and convex. Proof. An effective real sample bound gives a short-circuit radius R such that a nonempty input set S meets the closed ball of radius R. The intersection K is compact and convex. Thus 

χ(K) = 

{

0 K = ∅,

1 K̸ = ∅.

The Euler-parity algorithm of Theorem 7 decides which case holds in BPP C2P. This is why convexity helps: it makes a single bit of Euler characteristic detect nonemptiness. The algorithm need not recognize the closed-convex promise. For a rational symmetric pencil, the PSD condition has a polynomial-size semialgebraic description. If det( tI + A) = tm + c1tm−1 + · · · + cm, it is equivalent to cj ≥ 0 for every j. Indeed the roots are the negatives of the real eigenvalues, and a monic polynomial with nonnegative coefficients has no positive real root. Division-free determinant circuits compute all cj with polynomial size. The feasible set is closed and convex, so the preceding argument applies. Strict feasibility reduces to a closed SDP by adding variables δ, v and the constraints 

A(x) − δI ⪰ 0,

(δ 11 v

)

⪰ 0.

The second block forces δ > 0, and every positive-definite A(x) admits such a choice. There is also a useful equivalence independent of the CH arguments: 

Proposition 1. General rational SDP feasibility and strict rational SDP feasibility are polynomial-time many-one equivalent. The reduction from general to strict feasibility can send every NO input to a strongly infeasible pencil. The same assertion holds for second-order cone feasibility. 

13 The reverse reduction is the two-by-two block above. For the forward direction, first bound a possible feasible point by an effective radius M , then bound the positive infeasibility gap on [ −M − 1, M + 1] n by a small μ. Polynomially many two-by-two strict LMIs express M < T < M + 1 and 0 < ϵ < μ , even though the expanded binary descriptions of M and 1 /μ are exponential. The output is 

|xi| < T (1 ≤ i ≤ n), A(x) + ϵI ≻ 0.

Supplementary Section D proves the gadgets and the gap assertion, including weakly infeasible inputs. This use of large constants is related to [3, Theorem 9]; we make no priority claim for the reduction. Strict feasibility by itself does not justify a lower complexity bound than general feasibility. For a commuting rational pencil, simultaneous spectral decomposition does give a smaller bound: 

Commuting-SDP ∈ NP PosSLP ∩ coNP PosSLP ⊆ MA PP ∩ coMA PP ⊆ C2P. (11) The generated rational matrix algebra has dimension at most the matrix size. A separating element represents every coefficient matrix as a rational polynomial in one symmetric matrix. Its polynomially many distinct eigenvalues are specified by a dense squarefree polynomial and isolating intervals. Feasibility becomes a polynomial-size LP over these algebraic numbers. Active-constraint certificates for feasibility and sparse Farkas certificates for infeasibility are verified in PPosSLP . The first containment in (11) does not depend on our new arithmetic upper bounds. The second uses the PosSLP theorem below; fresh amplified oracle randomness gives NP BPP PP 

⊆ MA PP , and counting over Merlin’s strings after amplification gives MA PP ⊆ PP PP .The general SDP bound also extends to several compact coefficient encodings: rational arithmetic circuits, explicitly isolated dense algebraic numbers and arithmetic circuits over those leaves, and square roots of nonnegative integer-circuit values. Their graph constructions reduce them to ordinary rational SDP with polynomial matrix dimension; see Section D.3. This statement does not include arbitrary nested algebraic-root DAGs or succinct descriptions of exponentially sized matrices. 

# 5.2 PosSLP and coefficient positivity 

PosSLP asks whether an integer straight-line program using 0 , 1, +, −, × has positive output. The output may have exponentially many bits. A related problem asks for the sign of a specified coefficient of a polynomial given by a short arithmetic circuit, with the coefficient index in binary. These circuit problems are studied in [11, 12]. 

Theorem 5. Both problems belong to 

BPP PP ∩ PNP PP 

.

In particular they belong to C2P, by the randomized bound, and to PH PP , by the deterministic bound. 

The common input to both algorithms is an integer residue representative at every short modulus. For an SLP value this is direct evaluation. For a specified coefficient, use Kronecker encoding and Fourier extraction over a short Galois ring, with unit-character orthogonality valid modulo arbitrary prime powers. The resulting coefficient representative is a raw GapP PH expression; functional Toda–Watanabe absorption places the complete weighted counts in FP PP [14]. The beta identity (6) then converts the sign into a very small signed displacement on the circle. The deterministic algorithm asks whether any earlier dyadic scale has reached a fixed distance from zero. Binary search for the first such event uses NP PP ; its direction reveals the sign. The randomized algorithm locates a usable scale by the short-seed phase test of Lemma 6 and uses only a PP oracle. The complete independent proofs are in Sections H and I. They do not pass through the real-root selection machinery. The published general CH containment is due to [11]; the smaller bounds displayed here are the proposed refinements. The 2024 discussions in [16, 17] still cite the older upper bound; this comparison is not an exhaustive priority claim. Their relation to the ETR proof is arithmetic: modular representatives, weighted counting, and sign reconstruction are shared. There is no established reduction ∃R ⊆ NP PosSLP in this paper. Consequently the PosSLP theorem alone does not give the headline ETR theorem, or put ETR in PH PP .

14 5.3 Square-root sum 

The square-root-sum problem compares ∑ni=1 

√ai with an integer k, for binary nonnegative integers ai.Fixing either strict or nonstrict comparison gives the same upper bounds below. 

Corollary 2. 

SRS ∈ BPP PP ∩ PNP PP 

⊆ CH , SRS ∈ C2P.

Use the standard reduction to PosSLP from [11] and Theorem 5. Algebraic separation supplies a singly exponential precision bound. Newton approximations to the radicals attain that precision with polynomially many shared arithmetic-circuit operations; the separation bound distinguishes zero from either nonzero sign. The final integer comparison is a PosSLP instance. Thus this consequence improves the inherited fourth-level CH upper bound through the integer-sign algorithm; it does not require a separate real-feasibility reduction. A possible certificate route to MA ∩ coMA remains unresolved. Algebraic sums-of-squares existence is insufficient: the numerators and the identities to be checked need uniformly short, coherent descriptions. The four-square constructions, square-class promises, and the precise missing size hypothesis are kept in supplementary Sections L–O. They are not additional unconditional containments for SRS. 

# 5.4 Geometric real counting 

The standard name here is GCR , the geometric real counting class of Bürgisser and Cucker [2]. It is a function class: GCR = BP (# P0

> R

), where BP denotes the Boolean part of a constant-free real computation, not a bounded-error probability operator. Its basic total problem is pc( S) = 

{

|S| S is finite ,

∞ S is infinite ,

for a binary-described semialgebraic set. It counts distinct points, not connected components or algebraic multiplicities. The symbol ∞ is part of the output convention. 

Theorem 6. The total threshold problem {(S, k ) : pc( S) ≥ k} belongs to C4P. Consequently 

GCR ⊆ FP C4P, FP GCR ⊆ FP C4P.

Here is how counting avoids a fifth level. For a finite algebraic set Z, add a sphere coordinate to double its points and use a small compact sublevel of a sum of squares. After the weighted sextic perturbation of (5), Morse theory gives 2|Z| = N+ − N−,

where N+ and N− count critical points below the threshold with positive and negative Hessian determinant. The ordered labels of Section 3 give distinct addresses in a set of size H, a power of two exceeding the critical-algebra rank. Choose a uniform address. Accept at a positive critical point, reject at a negative one, and otherwise toss a fair coin. Its ideal acceptance probability is 12 + N+ − N−

2H = 12 + |Z|

H .

The local address and sign computations use BPP C3P, with error amplified far below 1 /H . Their randomness is sampled inside the outer PP C3P computation. A midpoint threshold therefore decides the desired integer comparison at level four. We do not place a new PP machine above a C4P predicate. Boolean combinations and inequalities admit a quadratic algebraic encoding with a known constant fiber size; divide out that size in the threshold. To recognize infinity, an effective one-block bound supplies η > 0below every nonzero squared distance between points of any finite input set. An infinite semialgebraic set contains an arc and therefore arbitrarily close distinct points. Hence 

S infinite ⇐⇒ ∃x, y ∈ S : 0 < ∥x − y∥2 < η. 

15 This is a polynomial-size ETR instance using a short dyadic circuit. Amplify its BPP C3P algorithm and merge its coins into the same threshold experiment. Finite counts have polynomially many output bits after quadratic encoding, so threshold queries recover the entire value in FP C4P. Section B supplies the geometric and error details. The published framework gives # P ⊆ GCC ⊆ GCR ⊆ FPSPACE , together with completeness results for point counting and Euler characteristic [2]. Our added ingredient is the signed Morse count combined with the norm and rank machinery. The full Euler characteristic and its emptiness flag also lie in FP C4P by those reductions. The lower bound for a few numerical Euler bits below is a different, more restricted output task. 

# 5.5 Euler characteristic and its low bits 

For arbitrary Boolean semialgebraic sets, use the additive, definable Euler characteristic χ∗. On locally closed sets this equals Euler characteristic with compact supports; on compact sets it equals ordinary Euler characteristic. 

Theorem 7. For every fixed c, the residue χ∗(S) mod 2 b, where 0 ≤ b ≤ c log 2(L + 2) and L is the input length, is computable in FBPP C2P. No finiteness, smoothness, or boundedness promise on S is needed. 

The first ingredient is a low-bit trick for locally qualified critical points. To compute (Nk

) mod 2, count increasing k-tuples, ordered by their distinct critical values. For polynomially bounded k, this is a single product-algebra parity problem with ordinary short-circuit sign guards. Its one norm has a raw GapP PP 

residue representative, so its sign is in BPP C2P. Lucas’s identity bit j (N ) = 

(N

2j

)

(mod 2) then recovers logarithmically many bits, since the required tuple lengths remain polynomial. Apply this to the positive and negative Hessian counts in the Morse formula. The second ingredient preserves Euler characteristic when converting an arbitrary Boolean semialgebraic set to compact algebraic sets. An algebraic lift Y has one independent sign choice per atom. A canonical guard chooses the nonnegative witness in each case, giving a semialgebraic bijection Ycan → S. Boolean and inverse witnesses make Ycan a union of connected components of Y , with a uniform sign gap on every compact truncation. Thus the canonical guard can be retained as a locally constant weight in the Morse calculation. Choose a short-circuit radius R beyond which both ball and sphere Euler characteristics stabilize, and put 

A = Y ∩ {∥ u∥ ≤ R}, B = Y ∩ {∥ u∥ = R}, D = {(u, z ) : u ∈ Y, ∥u∥2 + z2 = R2}.

The set Dcan doubles Acan along Bcan . Additivity and the contribution −1 of an open ray give 

χ∗(S) = χ(Dcan ) − χ(Bcan )2 . (12) Both terms in the numerator are Euler characteristics of selected components of compact algebraic sets. Compute them modulo 2 b+1 by the tuple-and-Morse algorithm, subtract, and divide the even residue by two. Only one extra bit is needed, independently of the number of original sign atoms. The canonical lift, the stabilization bound, and the Morse step are proved in Section C. There is no emptiness flag in Theorem 7. A nonempty circle has Euler characteristic zero, so Euler parity cannot decide general real feasibility. On the other hand, under a promise that |χ∗(S)| is polynomially bounded, enough low bits give the exact signed value in the same function class. In particular, finite sets of polynomially bounded cardinality can be counted in FBPP C2P, without recognizing that promise. 

# 5.6 Complex feasibility and dimension 

Let HN denote Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz as a decision problem: do given integer polynomial equations have a common complex zero? 

16 Theorem 8. 

HN ∈ BPP C2P ⊆ C3P.

The complex dimension of the zero set, with value −1 for the empty set, is in FBPP C2P.

This refinement has a separate dependency: it uses the published affine reduction of [10, Proposition 4.4] and the modular product construction, but not our ETR or integer-sign theorem. Quadratic wire encoding makes the input degrees at most two. The AGS reduction expresses nonemptiness through two trailing coefficient operations on resultants of explicitly given, constant-degree homogeneous forms, followed by evaluation at zero. One formal branch is computable in polynomial time at a short finite-field node. The flattened Poisson product costs one counting level, and joint Fourier extraction costs a second. Coefficient-prefix tests locate each first nonzero coefficient by binary search. Their geometric-sum weights fit inside the same Fourier sum. Random-prime identity testing distinguishes zero polynomials; fresh randomness and uniform degree and height bounds control the adaptive searches. Since only equality is needed, there is no residue-to-integer-sign step. Random affine sections then determine dimension with polynomially many calls to the same oracle. Section E records the precise interface and error analysis. 

# 5.7 Arithmetic extensions and stochastic fixed points 

Some further decision bounds follow by reductions, without changing the main norm argument. Adaptive polynomial-time PosSLP computations compile to one PosSLP query (Theorem 17). The same holds for the supported nested real-root gates of Theorem 18, including polynomial-degree principal roots; Theorem 23 extends this to binary-encoded root degrees and specified sparse-polynomial roots under its root-selection promises. Their sign problems therefore inherit both bounds in Theorem 5. The supplied domain and root-order promises are part of these statements. For the least-fixed-point threshold problems for branching stochastic systems treated in Proposition 15, the strategy certificates give 

NP PosSLP ∩ coNP PosSLP ⊆ MA PP ∩ coMA PP ⊆ C2P.

The underlying PPS reduction to PosSLP is published [25, 24]. The supplementary proofs state the precise systems, strategies, and comparison conventions. Conditional short-certificate proposals and restricted numerical reconstruction lemmas are discussed there as supporting material, rather than as stronger bounds for unrestricted ETR or square-root sum.

There is also a class consequence stated without arithmetic-circuit inputs. Let PP (pt ) 

> Q

denote comparison with a rational threshold of a sum of polynomial-time computable rational weights over polynomial-length witnesses, as in [4]. Each individual numerator and denominator has polynomial bit length; the denominator of their sum need not. Theorem 12 gives 

PP (pt ) 

> Q

⊆ BPP PP ∩ PNP PP 

, PPP (pt ) 

> Q

= PCoeffPosSLP .

Here CoeffPosSLP is specified coefficient positivity. An effective common-denominator bound reduces a nonzero rational sum to a small displacement with a known lower bound, so both sign-reconstruction algorithms apply directly. For the relation to coefficients, arithmetize the weight evaluator and replace the reciprocal denominator by a sufficiently long geometric sum, which has a short circuit. The coefficient construction preserves the threshold sign. The forward reduction is many-one; the reverse one asserted here is a polynomial-time Turing reduction. The full proof and the additional factorization promises are in Section K. 

17 Supplementary information 

The main text gives the containment statements in their current form. The following sections preserve the detailed arithmetic arguments, additional geometric interfaces, and certificate investigations. Intermediate numerical bounds are retained to explain which implementation choices cost levels. Conditional certificate hypotheses are explicitly separate from the proved implications that would use them. The finite checks supplied with the manuscript test identities and examples; they do not certify the asymptotic containment proofs. We first give details for the hierarchy and applications introduced above, then the longer homotopy, reconstruction, and certificate arguments. # A Resultant interfaces for the numerical real-hierarchy bound 

This section supplements the structural sampling argument in Section O.10. Its purpose is to justify the explicit ledger (10). The geometric invariant is unchanged: samples cover the required connected sign components, coordinate denominators are guarded at the selected root, and one finite infinitesimal tuple preserves the full representation formula. 

# A.1 Coefficient and value access 

Consider homogeneous forms of singly exponential degree in polynomially many variables, represented by indexed coefficients with polynomial-length addresses. Work modulo a short prime power in an unramified Galois ring whose residue field contains enough distinct reference nodes. Extension elements still have polynomial bit length. The reference system is a product of distinct linear factors in each variable, with unit Jacobian at every reference point. If the degrees are exponential, its values, derivatives, and Jacobian products use FP PP . Factorization and field metadata are found once at the charged preprocessing level; they are not hidden in a purported polynomial-time leaf evaluator. Interpolate formally from that reference system to the target. Polynomial Newton and inverse-Jacobian updates double the order of agreement with the formal branch at each step. There are only polynomially many updates to reach an exponential order. At a fixed short node the updates use polynomially many target value and derivative queries. Their untruncated polynomial degrees remain singly exponential. The Poisson formula expresses the resultant through one product indexed by the branches and the successive coordinate subsystems. Flatten these indices into one polynomial-length index. All homotopy and parameter coefficient extractions then form one joint Fourier sum, as in (32) and (33). If the target value oracle is at level r, branch work is at max( r, 1), the product at max( r, 1) + 1, and the final coefficient extraction at max( r, 1) + 2. If only coefficients at level h are supplied, their dense homogeneous monomial sum first gives a value oracle at h + 1. This proves the h + 3 coefficient interface and the value-interface refinement used in the main text. The formal Poisson/Newton identities are those of [10, Sections 5.2–5.3]; the finite-ring product implementation is that of Lemma 18. Homogeneous evaluation is substantive. For a coordinate map ( g0, g 1, . . . , g s), use the formal expression 

∑

> α

cαgE−| α|

> 0

∏

> i

gαi 

> i

, E ≥ deg P. 

Every exponent is nonnegative, so this is defined when g0 = 0. For a sign test at a valid affine point, choose 

E even and retain the separate condition g0̸ = 0. One must not replace the homogeneous evaluator by affine division at all Fourier nodes. 

# A.2 Restriction without adaptive degree truncation 

To check (8), homogenize in two variables. At a specialization of degree d, the degree-D form of f + t has the factor XD−d 

> 0

. Evaluating the other form at infinity gives V , so resultant multiplicativity extracts V D−d. The 

18 constant V -coefficient of what remains is 

aE−ed Res d,e (f + t, g + λt ),

where ad̸ = 0 is the leading coefficient of f , and e is the degree of g + λt over the parameter field. Its leading factor is independent of t. The even choice of E removes the harmless ordering sign from the infinity factor. Nonzero constants and zero defining polynomials are handled as stated after (8). At a common root with multiplicities a, b , deform the two local equations by t and λt . Their local resultant has order min( a, b ). For a = b, its leading coefficient is a nonzero polynomial in the independent parameter λ; keeping λ formal prevents cancellation. Adding over the roots gives the gcd degree. Retaining the original coefficient arrays and all coefficients in V, t, λ therefore retains the actual degrees and required gcd degrees. For completeness, these data have the needed geometric meaning. Near any parameter point of a connected sign cell, choose disjoint small neighborhoods of the distinct roots. Constancy of degree and of the number of distinct roots forbids a split or collision. Conjugation and local uniqueness keep a real root real. The local gcd contribution of each branch with a guard is upper semicontinuous; constancy of the total forces each contribution to be locally constant. A guard is consequently either zero along a branch or continuously nonzero with fixed real sign. Continuation gives the same conclusion on the connected cell. This is the branch property required by the BPR sampling construction. 

# A.3 Merged derivative extraction and flattening 

Suppose a sampling resultant has a Fourier representation qi = |U |−1 ∑ 

> z∈U

P (z)z−i, with any other Fourier variables suppressed. Substitute this expression into the homogeneous derivative sum and interchange the two finite sums. The result is exactly 

|U |−1 ∑

> z∈U

P (z)KD,μ (A, B, z ).

The sampling-product value P (z) is already at level h + 2, whereas the kernel is at level two. Thus the value stays at h + 3. Higher derivative order and selection of an infinitesimal coefficient change the kernel or add a character weight in the same sum. This proves the saving of one level per real block. The two extra homogeneous coefficient compositions in the ledger are each charged two levels, even where an implementation might save more. One common finite specialization is charged once. Flat recoding evaluates its regularized tuple system at k + 1, takes its sampling resultant at k + 3, and composes coordinates at k + 5. Write ℓ = k + 5. The full Thom-hash selector has a homogeneous value at ℓ + 2; the defining polynomial and its first derivative are at ℓ + 1. Put the selector last in the binary resultant. Its derivative is then unnecessary: only the first polynomial defines the continued roots. The value interface gives the guarded parity polynomial at ℓ + 4. Two root representations cost eight levels, the inherited sign algorithm costs one, and fixed Boolean alternation with relativized Toda costs two. This is k + 5 + 8 + 1 + 2, as claimed. The complete representation formula before specialization uses only a dimension depending on w, although it has exponentially many indexed coefficients. Fixed-dimensional elimination therefore gives singly exponential bounds for all its cumulative projection polynomials. The leading-monomial choice of the ϵi in Section O.10 applies simultaneously to validity, coverage, and parent–child coordinate equalities. Hashing is applied after this choice. No lower real bound is asserted for a value that remains formally infinitesimal. 

# B Compact Morse counts and total point counting 

# B.1 A topology-preserving weighted critical system 

Let a compact algebraic set Z be the zero set of a coercive quartic sum of squares F . The examples in the main text use a sphere equation, so coercivity follows from F (x) ≥ (R2 − ∥ x∥2)2. There are finitely many critical values. A one-block existential description of those values and the effective degree and height bounds of [2, Section 4.1] give a positive short dyadic ϵ below every positive critical value. 

19 For sufficiently small ϵ, the compact sublevel K = {F ≤ ϵ} strongly deformation retracts to Z. One direct justification uses the gradient inequality near the compact zero set, ∥∇ F ∥ ≥ cF θ , with θ < 1. Negative gradient flow enters every lower positive sublevel in uniformly bounded time and its remaining length from level a is at most a1−θ /(c(1 − θ)). The endpoint maps therefore converge uniformly, giving a continuous retraction fixing Z. A time rescaling gives the strong deformation retraction. These qualitative constants prove the topological assertion; they are not algorithmic inputs. Put W (x) = ∑ 

> i

Otherwise its squared minimum is an endpoint of a one-block semialgebraic image, giving a short dyadic lower bound by the same effective estimates. Bound W and ∇W on K by AW , B W , and choose a power of two Q such that 

AW /(6 Q) < ϵ/ 4, BW /(6 Q) < γ/ 2.

Also make its two-adic valuation exceed the critical-system requirement in Section 3. Throughout 0 ≤ t ≤ 1/(6 Q), the boundary F + tW = ϵ lies in the positive regular band and has gradient norm at least γ/ 2. The vector field −W ∇(F + tW )/∥∇ (F + tW )∥2, with a compact cutoff, moves these boundaries and their sublevels by an isotopy. Consequently J = 6 QF + W , at threshold T = 6 Qϵ , has the same sublevel Euler characteristic as Z. The two-adic construction makes J a proper Morse function with distinct critical values. Hence

The bounds here permit already long integer constants: the effective coefficient bit-height estimate is singly exponential in dimension and degree and has polynomial dependence on the incoming bit height. Alternatively, when deriving an initial syntactic bound, introduce unique quadratic powering wires before applying the estimate. Neither argument applies a polynomial-size theorem to a secretly doubly exponential expanded input. 

# B.2 Finite and infinite point counts 

For a finite zero set Z, an isolated-point radius bound gives R strictly above the norms of all its points. The zero set of ∑

> i

fi(x)2 + ( R2 − ∥ x∥2 − z2)2

has exactly two points over each member of Z. Thus (13) is N+ − N− = 2 |Z|; there is no assertion of one critical point per zero. For a Boolean input with a strict sign atoms, the algebraic lift in the next section, without its canonical guard, has exactly 2 a points over each original point. The same experiment therefore has ideal acceptance probability 1 /2 + 2 a|S|/H . Its threshold for |S| ≥ k is 

tk = 12 + 2ak − 1/2

H .

The signed norm tests and the common normalizer can each be amplified to error at most 1 /(256 H), using polynomially many repetitions because log H is polynomial. Their combined error is below 3 /(256 H), much smaller than the 1 /(2 H) midpoint gap. All random bits are internal to one PP C3P experiment. Ordinary PP padding implements comparison with the short dyadic tk.For total counting, consider the one-block image 

DS = {t > 0 : ∃x, y ∈ S, t = ∥x − y∥2}.

An effective coefficient bit bound B for its quantifier-free description is chosen from the original input, before adding any new precision constant. If S is finite, every member of DS is a zero of a nonzero description 

20 polynomial; otherwise the truth value would hold on a neighborhood. Removing a zero power and applying the reciprocal Cauchy bound gives t ≥ 2−B−2. Set η = 2 −B−3. If S is infinite, a positive-dimensional semialgebraic cell supplies pairs at arbitrarily small distance. This proves the infinity test in the main text. Amplify that test to error at most 1 /(256 H). Accept immediately when it reports infinity and otherwise run the finite experiment. For finite inputs the midpoint gap survives; for infinite inputs the acceptance probability is at least 1 − 1/(256 H). In the nontrivial finite threshold range tk ≤ 1 − 1/(2 H), so the infinite case also answers correctly. Thresholds below zero are immediate, and thresholds beyond the uniform finite bound use the infinity test alone. The finite experiment remains a syntactically defined computation even on infinite inputs, so its rare execution on the wrong branch creates no promise-oracle problem. 

# C Euler residues for arbitrary Boolean semialgebraic sets 

# C.1 An algebraic lift with a canonical component selection 

Replace comparisons by Boolean combinations of strict atoms p > 0. Give every arithmetic gate and every atom value, including constants, its own unique evaluation coordinate. For each atom introduce b, c, t, s, v, w 

and the equations 

b(b − 1) = 0 , b(pt 2 − 1) = 0 , (1 − b)t = 0 ,

(1 − b)( s2 + p) = 0 , bs = 0 , c(c − 1) = 0 ,cp = 0 , (1 − c)( pv − 1) = 0 , cv = 0 ,c(w2 − 1) = 0 , (1 − c)w = 0 .

Encode the Boolean gates uniquely and require the output to be one. Use unique wires to reduce every displayed equation to degree two. Let Y be the resulting algebraic set. When p > 0, the atom has b = 1 , c = 0 , v = 1 /p , t = ±1/√p, and s = w = 0. When p < 0, it has 

b = c = 0 , v = 1 /p , s = ±√−p, and t = w = 0. When p = 0, it has b = 0 , c = 1 , v = 0, w = ±1, and t = s = 0. There are exactly two witnesses in every case. The guard t, s, w ≥ 0 for every atom retains exactly one, so the projection Ycan → S is a semialgebraic bijection and preserves additive Euler characteristic. The Boolean coordinates are constant on each connected component of Y . In a fixed truth and zero case, exactly one of t, s, w is active and never zero; its sign cannot change on a component. Thus Ycan is a union of components, closed and open in Y . On ∥u∥ ≤ R, both p and its inverse coordinate v, when active, have magnitude at most R. Each nonzero canonical witness therefore has magnitude at least R−1/2. For ∆ = 1 /(4 R), the shifted strict guard uj > −∆/2 agrees with the canonical choice in a sufficiently small neighborhood of this compact portion. It correctly retains an inactive zero witness. 

# C.2 One radius stabilizes both the ball and its boundary 

Let F = ∑ f 2 

> i

for the quadratic equations of Y , with ordinary polynomial-bit coefficients. Consider the set in the ( e, t )-plane given by 

e > 0, t ≥ 0, ∃x, λ : F (x) = e, ∥x∥2 = t, x = λ∇F (x).

For a regular value e, these are the radial critical values on the smooth hypersurface F = e. There are finitely many of them by Sard’s theorem and semialgebraicity. There are also only finitely many exceptional critical values of F . The planar set thus has dimension at most one. One-block elimination gives a defining family of bivariate polynomials with degree and coefficient bit height bounded by H = 2 poly( L). Every point of this planar set is a zero of one of its nonzero defining polynomials, since otherwise membership would persist on a neighborhood. Write such a polynomial as 

eaQ(e, t ) with Q(0 , t )̸ ≡ 0. Any finite limiting radial critical value as e ↓ 0 is a root of some Q(0 , t ), and hence has magnitude at most 1 + 2 H . Choose a short power-of-two R with R2 > 2 + 2 H .Fix R ≤ r1 < r 2 < ∞. For all sufficiently small positive regular e, the hypersurface F = e has no radial tangency in this closed annulus; otherwise a convergent sequence would violate the preceding bound. Proper-submersion isotopy for the smooth tube {F ≤ e}, including its boundary, makes both its sphere 

21 sections and its ball truncations topologically constant between r1 and r2. At the two endpoints, compact semialgebraic small-sublevel retraction identifies their Euler characteristics with those of Y . This applies even when Y is singular. The robust component guard above is locally constant on a sufficiently small tube in a slightly larger compact ball, so the isotopy and endpoint retractions also preserve the selected components. Both Euler functions therefore stabilize beyond the explicitly chosen R. Qualitative conic structure at some possibly larger radius gives χ∗(Ycan ) = χ(Acan ) − χ(Bcan ); stabilization brings this numerical equality back to R. The double identity χ(Dcan ) = 2 χ(Acan )−χ(Bcan ) proves (12). Bounding a deformation-retraction radius alone would not justify the boundary term, which is why both Euler functions were controlled. 

# C.3 Tuple parity, Lucas’s identity, and signed division 

For a fixed local qualification, let N count the corresponding real critical points of the simple system. In its 

k-fold product, add the strict inequalities J(x(1) ) < · · · < J (x(k)). Distinct critical values give exactly (Nk

)

real tuples. A rational sign filter for the conjunction, followed by positive denominator clearing and a small nonvanishing shift, gives a norm whose sign is ( −1) (Nk ). For polynomial k, the tuple system has polynomial dimension, and its formal branch values are polynomial-time computable at short nodes. The product costs 

FP PP , the raw Fourier extraction is GapP PP , and randomized sign reconstruction uses BPP C2P. No rank norm occurs in this calculation. Lucas’s identity recovers bit j by choosing k = 2 j . For b ≤ c log 2(L + 2), every required tuple has polynomial size. Apply these tests to both Hessian signs in (13), retaining the robust component guard. This computes the two compact Euler terms in (12) modulo 2 b+1 . Their difference has an even canonical residue r

in [0 , 2b+1 ). Return r/ 2. This is correct even when the integer Euler characteristic is negative. Polynomial amplification makes the whole output correct with probability at least two thirds.

After unique-wire quadratization, apply [10, Proposition 4.4]. It constructs polynomially many families of homogeneous forms Gi,j of degree at most two in the geometric variables, degree at most n in w, and degree at most one in t, u , such that with high probability the original variety is nonempty precisely when, for some

Their values and first derivatives are polynomial-time computable at short field nodes. Use a homotopy parameter s distinct from t. Uniform degree and integer coefficient bit bounds apply to every prefix tested, before any adaptive search. A nonzero coefficient of bit length at most H has at most H distinct prime divisors. Sample a polynomial-bit prime from an interval with many more than H/δ primes and lower endpoint above the degree bound divided by δ. Capped rejection sampling and polynomial-time primality testing suffice. Except with probability 

O(δ), reduction preserves a nonzero coefficient; Schwartz–Zippel over the same field then detects the nonzero polynomial. Zero polynomials always evaluate to zero. Binary search for the first nonzero prefix takes polynomially many tests in t, then in w. Conditional on all previous answers being correct, each next test has the same worst-case error bound. Fresh randomness and a union bound therefore control the adaptive search and final evaluation at u = 0. Combined with the AGS reduction error, amplification yields BPP C2P. No sign of a large integer is queried. For dimension, a generic affine subspace of codimension k meets a nonempty complex variety exactly when its dimension is at least k. Standard degree bounds control the bad affine choices, so polynomial-bit random coefficients suffice. Test the polynomially many possible k, using fresh amplified HN calls, and first test emptiness. This returns the dimension in FBPP C2P.

# F Arithmetic conventions and the first-event test 

We use the input bit length n throughout. An integer or an index is short if it has polynomially many bits. A numerical bound of size 2 poly( n) is singly exponential, and itself has a short binary encoding. In particular, an integer of 2 poly( n) bits is much larger than a short integer. We always distinguish a value from the binary description of a bound on its length. An SLP of size s, with constants of magnitude at most 2 b, has output magnitude less than 2 H , where we may take 

H = ( b + 1)2 s+1 . (15) Indeed, a maximum magnitude M ≥ 2 grows by at most squaring at each gate. The same argument applies to the coefficient ℓ1-norm of a polynomial SLP. Its total degree is at most 2 s, and each coefficient satisfies (15). The class # PA consists of accepting-path counts of polynomial-time nondeterministic machines with oracle A, and GapP A consists of differences of two such counts. An exponentially indexed sum of short nonnegative FP A values is in # PA: guess an index, and create as many accepting branches as the value at that index. Signed sums are in GapP A. These classes are closed under sums over polynomial-length indices and under products of polynomially many factors, when their machines and length bounds are uniform. A short count can be recovered in FP PP A

through threshold queries. This last step is a function computation; arbitrary postprocessing of the result need not remain a counting function. For a monic polynomial q, write 

q[j](z) = [ uj ]q(z + u)for its Hasse derivative. Since q(j) = j!q[j], the two have the same signs at real inputs. We use Hasse derivatives so that our integer construction can later be reduced at small primes without requiring an inverse of j!. For an element a of a finite free algebra A, Norm A(a) is the determinant of multiplication by a. Over an algebraically closed field this is the product of its values at the points of the algebra, with local algebra dimensions as multiplicities. These identities hold even when the algebra is not reduced. We use determinants to prove identities and height bounds; we do not expand an exponentially large determinant as our algorithm. 

# F.1 Detecting the first displacement 

The following elementary observation will supply the final sign test. Its statement is independent of the oracle used to compute a summand.

Before an event, the true centered distance from an integer is less than 1 /8 + ϵ. Its double has magnitude less than 1 /4 + 2 ϵ < 1/2, so it does not wrap around the circle. Starting with δ, induction shows that the centered value is exactly 2 j δ up to and including the first event. At that event its magnitude is at least 1 /8 − ϵ, and at most 1 /4 + 2 ϵ; at index zero the stronger initial upper bound suffices. Hence the sign is positive if vj < 1/2 and negative if vj > 1/2. If δ = 0, there is no event. Otherwise there must be one by J: the no-wrap induction would give a centered value 2 J δ of magnitude at least one. All multipliers in (16) are polynomial-time computable from the binary index j, by modular powering. The whole sum has polynomially many bits because it has exponentially many polynomial-bit summands. For example, if individual event tests are in PA, the predicate that some event occurs by t is in NP A. Binary search on this monotone prefix predicate finds the first event with polynomially many queries. Alternatively, guessing the first event and universally excluding earlier ones puts the sign test in Σ A 

> 2

. We will use the deterministic search for PosSLP , and the quantified version when combining it with the existential choices in ETR. 

# G Detailed shared-homotopy proof for real feasibility 

We now construct an integer whose sign can certify a real solution. The critical system is fixed deterministically, and its objective separates all critical points. The integer depends only on a short guessed list of sign conditions; every negative answer certifies a real solution.

27 G.2 The objective separates its critical points 

The large power of two needed for the minimum gap also gives deterministic separation. No generic parameter or projection is needed. 

Lemma 9. All D complex critical points of Jw are simple, and their objective values are pairwise distinct. None has objective value T .Proof. Every derivative of the integer sum of squares F has even coefficients. Modulo two the critical equations are copies of 

p(U ) = U 5 + U + 1 = ( U 2 + U + 1)( U 3 + U 2 + 1) .

The factors are irreducible over F2, and the five roots are distinct in F64 . Squarefreeness also follows from 

U p ′(U ) = 1 in F2[U ]/(p). The monic standard basis makes A finite free over Z. Its Jacobian norm is odd, hence nonzero, so every characteristic-zero point is simple. Equivalently, A ⊗ Z2 is finite étale : in the unramified degree-six ring of integers, the 5 m residue tuples lift uniquely and exhaust the points. Compare two distinct lifted points x, y , with v2(2) = 1. If xj ≡ yj (mod 2), the divided difference 

p(xj ) − p(yj )

xj − yj

≡ p′(xj )̸ ≡ 0 (mod 2) is a unit (interpreted polynomially if the coordinates coincide). The critical equations and the even coefficients of FXj therefore give 

v2(xj − yj ) ≥ v2(Q0) − (j − 1) + 1 .

Set H(U ) = U 6 + 3( U + 1) 2. At a root of p modulo two, H(U ) = U + 1. If i is the first coordinate with different residues, the i-th term of 

Jw(x) − Jw(y) = 6 Q0(F (x) − F (y)) + ∑

> j

2j−1(H(xj ) − H(yj ))

has valuation exactly i − 1. Every earlier summand has valuation at least v2(Q0) + 1, every later summand has valuation at least i, and the F term has valuation at least v2(Q0) + 1. Thus v2(Jw(x) − Jw(y)) = i − 1, proving separation. This is an algebraic nonvanishing statement, so it holds over C as well as over an algebraic closure of Q2.Finally A0 = 91(2 m − 1) + 1 is even, so T is even. Modulo two, Jw − T = X1 + 1, which is a unit since 

p(1) = 1. Its norm is odd, and it cannot vanish at any complex point. The integral pure-monic presentation ensures that reduction modulo two detects every component. Reduction of an arbitrary nonmonic system would not justify this proof. In particular, the critical points have distinct values and the real global minimum is unique. Define 

q(z) = Norm A(z − Jw), g(z) = z − T. (21) The integer polynomial q is monic of degree D and squarefree. Its roots are exactly the objective values of the complex critical points. A real root α comes from one such point xα; conjugation fixes this unique point, so it is real. Consequently 

g(α) = Jw(xα) − T. (22) A root with g < 0 certifies feasibility. Conversely, the global minimum of every feasible instance supplies such a root. We call these roots qualifying . None has g = 0. 

# G.3 A short list of signs isolates a root 

At the simple real roots of q, the vectors 

(sgn q[1] (α), . . . , sgn q[D](α))

28 are distinct. Here is the usual Thom argument in the form we need. A sign condition on derivatives of orders 

j + 1 , . . . , D defines an interval, by downward induction. On that interval q(j) is monotone, so imposing its sign leaves an interval. On an interval with the full derivative sign vector of a simple root, q′ has fixed nonzero sign. The polynomial cannot have two roots there. Positive factorial scaling gives the same conclusion for Hasse derivatives. 

Lemma 10. In a nonempty collection of at most D distinct ternary vectors, one vector can be isolated by fixing at most ⌈log 2 D⌉ coordinates. Proof. If several vectors remain, choose a coordinate on which they differ and retain its smallest nonempty sign class. At most half remain. Repeat until one remains. For example, among (+ , +) , (+ , −), (−, 0), requiring a negative first coordinate already isolates the third vector. The lemma does not promise a short description of every prescribed vector. Existence of one is exactly what the decision problem needs. Let κ = ⌈log 2 D⌉. Guess a list of at most κ pairs ( ji, σ i), with 1 ≤ ji ≤ D and σi ∈ {− 1, 0, 1}. The empty list is allowed. Applied to the qualifying roots, the lemma shows that some such list selects exactly one if any exist. An arbitrary list may select several, which will cause no soundness problem. 

# G.4 Turning the list into a polynomial selector 

We next need a rational function that behaves like sign on every nonzero algebraic value in the list. Effective bounds are easy to obtain without isolating any roots. 

Lemma 11. Let q ∈ Z[z] be monic of degree D, with coefficients of magnitude at most 2B , and let h ∈ Z[z]

Proof. Cauchy’s bound gives |α| ≤ 2B+1 , proving the upper estimate. In Res z (q(z), h (z) + v) = ∏

the lowest nonzero coefficient of v is the product of all the nonzero values, including multiplicities. This is a nonzero integer, so its magnitude is at least one. Bound all factors except the chosen one by the upper estimate. The polynomials in (21) have singly exponential degree and coefficient bit lengths. To see the height bound, reduce a polynomial of degree at most six times a standard monomial modulo the pure-monic equations. Each reduction strictly lowers total degree; a reduction path has O(m) steps and polynomial branching. The multiplication-matrix entries have singly exponential bit lengths. The determinant estimate then multiplies these lengths by at most D, adding O(D log D) for the number of determinant terms. This is a bound, not a determinant algorithm. Hasse derivatives increase coefficient magnitudes by at most 2 D .Lemma 11 therefore gives a short, singly exponential L ≥ 1 such that all nonzero values g(α) and q[j](α)lie between 2 −L and 2 L in magnitude. The same L can be chosen uniformly for all allowed parameters and lists.

# G.5 A nonzero norm and its sign 

The polynomial V may vanish at nonreal roots of q. If we simply took its resultant with q, those zeros would erase the real information. Apply Lemma 11 once more, now to V , and let βV be its upper-value exponent. The short choice K∗ = Dβ V + 2 ensures 2 K∗ |V (α)| > 2 at every nonzero value. Put 

U = 2 K∗ V + 1 , Z = Norm A(U (Jw)) = Res z (q, U ). (25) At a zero of V , U equals one. Elsewhere its magnitude cannot be zero. At real roots its sign is unchanged. The integer Z is therefore nonzero. Nonreal conjugate roots contribute positive factors U (α)U (α) = |U (α)|2, with the appropriate multiplic-ities. Multiple real roots also contribute positively, since they have g = 0. The only negative factors are selected qualifying simple real roots. Consequently sgn Z = ( −1) #{selected qualifying roots }. (26) This proves the two directions of the reduction separately. If any permitted guess makes Z < 0, there is a qualifying root, hence a real critical point with Jw < T , and the original instance is feasible. If the instance is feasible, choose a short list isolating one qualifying root of the fixed system. Its norm is negative. We have proved ETR-INV instance feasible ⇐⇒ ∃ list of polynomial length with Z < 0. (27) 

30 All degree and height estimates above are uniform. For a polynomial of degree E, a monic reduction path has at most E + 4 m steps. Singly exponential E therefore still gives singly exponential coefficient bit lengths in the final multiplication matrix. The final norm adds at most another singly exponential factor to this logarithmic height. There is therefore a known short integer H such that 0 < |Z| < 2H , H ≤ 2poly( n). (28) The constants used to define Z have polynomial-size SLPs, but this does not give such an SLP for the whole norm. We next give the modular algorithm that its implicit description does support.

The intermediate systems need no real interpretation. We only use their polynomial identities. There is a uniform singly exponential degree bound for Z(t). If a polynomial in the quotient has X-degree 

E and coefficient t-degree L, a multiplication-matrix entry starts at total X-degree at most E + 4 m. A monic reduction lowers this degree and adds at most one power of t. Lemma 13. For each prime p > 5 and grid point a, a uniform polynomial-size SLP over Fp computes a polynomial vector Ba(t) which agrees modulo tK+1 with the formal branch of (29) starting at a. The approximant has no variable denominators. Its syntactic degree has a uniform singly exponential bound independent of the values of the field constants. Proof. Write X = a + v. The initial Jacobian is diagonal, with nonzero entries h′

> i

(ai), so the equations become a proper system v = Pa(t, v ), with Pa(0 , 0) = 0 and ( Pa)v (0 , 0) = 0. Its coefficients use only inverses of the nonzero field constants h′

> i

(ai). Start with v0 = 0, which agrees with the solution modulo t. At an approximant vr with vr(0) = 0, the matrix Ar = ( Pa)v (t, v r) is divisible by t. In a Newton correction, replace the inverse Jacobian by the polynomial (I − Ar )−1 ≡ I + Ar + · · · + AKr (mod tK+1 ).

Matrix powering and geometric summation take polynomially many gates, since the matrices have dimension 

m and K is given in binary. The polynomial Taylor identity gives doubling of precision. Thus O(log( K + 1)) iterations suffice. No truncation of an expanded polynomial is performed. The resulting SLP computes a possibly higher-degree polynomial with the required congruence. A coarse recurrence ∆ r+1 ≤ C(K + 1)(∆ r + 1) bounds its syntactic degree by 2 O((log( K+1)) 2). These constructions are uniform in the grid index and modulus. Over Fp[[ t]], evaluation on the exact formal branches identifies the completed quotient with a product of 

D copies of Fp[[ t]]. The evaluation matrix is a tensor Vandermonde matrix at t = 0, hence its determinant is a unit. Thus norms are products over the branches. Use the same approximants Ba in every inner and outer norm. Coefficient extraction in an auxiliary variable, polynomial substitution, and the cleared filter operations all preserve congruence modulo tK+1 . This 

31 includes substituting an approximate outer branch into an approximate inner norm. The resulting polynomial 

P (t) therefore satisfies 

P (t) ≡ Z(t) (mod tK+1 ). (31) Thus the same branch family computes both norms. The argument uses formal polynomial congruences, so it requires no analytic continuation of individual roots through collisions. Choose a short prime p with p − 1 greater than K, the syntactic degree bounds of all approximate polynomials, and all auxiliary extraction indices. The order of these choices is important: bound the exact degree, choose the precision, bound the approximate circuit degrees, and only then choose the field. The degree bounds depend on the circuit shapes, so this is not circular.

# G.8 Recovering the integer sign 

We can now recover the sign of Z within a fixed CH level. Choose a short interval of primes above the preceding degree threshold whose product M satisfies M > 2H+4 , using the height bound (28). For completeness, fixed effective Chebyshev constants give c1x ≤ ϑ(x) ≤ c2x, where ϑ(x) = ∑

Both endpoints are short, and H1 = X⌈log 2 X⌉ + 1 bounds log 2 M . We index integers in this interval and discard composites by primality testing; we never need an algorithm for the indexed prime.
