The displacement has magnitude below 1 /8, and, when nonzero, greater than 2 −H1 . Lemma 7 applies. Each coarse sum costs one additional counting level. A short existential choice of the first event, followed by a universal check of earlier indices, gives the sign. Adding a fixed number of Boolean quantifiers above a fixed CH oracle remains within CH. 

Proof of Theorem 1. Guess the derivative-sign list in (27). The preceding construction decides whether its scalar is negative in a fixed level of CH, uniformly over all guesses. Merge these guesses with the first-event existential quantifier. Soundness and completeness are exactly those established in (27). This proves the containment. The proof has retained the real information in the sign of the norm, while computing that norm through complex formal branches. We next improve the sign reconstruction for integers with a simpler modular representation, then return to the level used by this real construction. 

# H All-moduli representatives and deterministic sign reconstruction 

We now turn to the sign of an integer given by a polynomial-size arithmetic circuit. Modular evaluation is easy, but the integer can have exponentially many bits. The difficulty is to recover its sign without constructing those bits or introducing another counting oracle at each reconstruction step. We use a rational identity whose value is positive and extremely small. Multiplying this identity by the unknown integer encodes its sign as the direction of a small displacement from an integer. The weights in the identity are binomial coefficients of exponential size. We only need representatives of these weights modulo short integers. Our first lemma produces such representatives as counting functions. Their values need not be canonical residues; this flexibility is what permits the subsequent weighted sum to remain a single counting computation. 

# H.1 Coefficient representatives over finite rings 

Lemma 14. Let f ∈ Z[X1, . . . , X r] be given by a division-free integer SLP. Let D bound every individual degree, and let 0 ≤ kj ≤ D. There is a uniform #P function W (f, D, k, m ) satisfying 

W (f, D, k, m ) ≡ [Xk]f (mod m)

for every positive integer m. The parameters D, k, m are encoded in binary. In particular, the representative has polynomially many bits in the complete input length, even when the coefficient has exponentially many bits. Proof. The case m = 1 is immediate. We first construct a representative modulo one maximal prime-power factor pa of m. Choose a prime d > max(2 , ⌈log 2(D + 2) ⌉), with d̸ = p. Scanning two successive Bertrand intervals and testing primality finds such a d in polynomial time. Put Q = pd. Then Q − 1 > D , and d, log Q,and a log p are polynomially bounded. We average over a family of field presentations instead of choosing one. Let I consist of the monic irreducible polynomials of degree d over Fp whose Y d−1 coefficient is zero. Its cardinality is 

I0 = pd−1 − 1

d .

Indeed, there are pd−1 trace-zero elements in FQ. Since d is prime, an element of smaller degree lies in Fp; its trace is d times the element, so only zero has trace zero. The remaining elements occur in orbits of size d.

33 Furthermore, dI 0 ≡ − 1 (mod p). Thus I0 is a unit modulo pa, and both I0 and its inverse are computable in polynomial time. Membership in I is testable in polynomial time. For each F ∈ I , lift its coefficients to {0, . . . , p − 1} and form 

A = ( Z/p aZ)[ Y ]/(F (Y )) .

This is the unramified Galois ring GR( pa, d ). An element has d coordinates modulo pa, in the basis 1, Y, . . . , Y d−1, so ring arithmetic and binary powering take polynomial time. An element is a unit precisely when its reduction modulo p is nonzero. Indeed, a residue-field inverse lifts to a product of the form 1 + pv ,and a finite geometric series in pv corrects this product to 1. r, log( D + 2) , log m, as required. The lemma supplies a nonnegative representative even for a negative coefficient. Only its congruence matters. In particular, the proof makes no assertion that reducing a counting-function value to its canonical residue preserves the counting class. The extension ring serves two separate purposes. Its residue field provides more interpolation points than the formal degree, while its characteristic retains the requested prime-power modulus. The interpolation length is Q − 1, which is coprime to the characteristic. Thus increasing the field degree avoids aliasing without introducing a noninvertible averaging factor. The eventual coefficient belongs to the base ring. Averaging over presentations is valid because this scalar is independent of the presentation; arbitrary intermediate coordinate values would not have this property. 

# H.2 A beta kernel for integer signs 

We isolate the reconstruction step so that its oracle requirement is explicit. Here GapP PH means that a fixed finite level of PH suffices for the oracle computation. The next statement is useful beyond SLPs. Its hypothesis specifies an integer through compatible congruences, together with a height bound. The algorithm need not know a compact arithmetic expression for that integer. What matters is the complexity of a representative for each congruence. The representative may depend on the modulus in any uniform way, and it may be signed. We impose no joint size bound on the integer reconstructed from all the congruences except the stated height bound. 

Lemma 15. Suppose an integer family Z(x) has a known bound |Z(x)| < 2H(x), where H(x) ≥ 1 is polynomial-time computable in binary and has polynomially many bits. Suppose a uniform function R(x, m ) ∈ GapP PH 

satisfies

h(y, 0m)/2p(|y|)⌋

(mod 2 m) for all y, m. (37) This is an explicit block-extraction form of the functional Toda–Watanabe reduction [14]. When deriving (37) from the amplification construction, one uses modulus 2 m+p(|y|) before the final shift. Choose m above the polynomial output-bit bound of f . The block then recovers f (y) exactly from one # P value on a polynomial-size input. Toda’s inclusion PH ⊆ BPP ⊕P [13] gives the # PPH special case. Taking a difference handles GapP. Consequently 

GapP PH ⊆ FP #P = FP PP .

This is an exact functional reduction; the language containment PH ⊆ P#P alone would not justify the step. All the parameters supplied to this reduction are short. In particular, y includes the binary scale j, rather than a unary string of length j. The unary padding 0 m in (37) is polynomial because it bounds the output length of Tj , not the output length of Z(x). For a signed count, the two nonnegative parts have polynomial bounds from their counting machines, so the same reasoning applies to each part before subtraction. We can therefore evaluate vj , the event predicate dist( vj , Z) ≥ 1/8, and its side vj < 1/2, in PPP . Lemma 7 shows that there is no event precisely when Z(x) = 0, and otherwise the first event has the sign of δ. Its hypotheses hold with the signed weights Ai, precision b, and search bound J just established. This invokes the no-wrap argument of that lemma, including its treatment of zero and of an event at the initial scale. To locate that event, consider the monotone prefix predicate Prefix( x, t ) ⇐⇒ ∃ j ≤ t dist( vj , Z) ≥ 1/8, 0 ≤ t ≤ J. 

The guessed index has polynomial length and its verification is in PPP , so Prefix belongs to NP PP . We first query Prefix at J to detect zero. Otherwise, O(log( J + 1)) binary-search queries find the least event; its side is also accessible to the same oracle. Finally, CN > 0 implies sgn δ = sgn Z(x). The order of the operations is important. We first form the counting function in (36), using the representatives only through sums and products. We then retrieve its short value and perform ordinary deterministic arithmetic. Thus canonical reduction occurs after the counting sum, where it does not add a counting level. The fixed precision does not discard the information needed for the sign. It identifies a scale at which that information becomes visible. The first-event lemma makes this scale search reliable, while the prefix oracle makes it efficient: the number of possible scales is exponential, but their binary-search depth is polynomial. These two facts account for the different roles of PP and the outer NP oracle in the conclusion. 

36 H.3 Applying the sign criterion to arithmetic circuits 

Proof of the deterministic assertion in Theorem 5. Let the input SLP have s arithmetic gates, and choose 

β ≥ 1 so that every input integer constant has magnitude at most 2 β . Starting with M0 = 2 β , each arithmetic gate raises a valid bound on the maximum magnitude to at most its square: addition and subtraction use 2M ≤ M 2, and multiplication uses M 2. Hence its integer output satisfies 

|Z| ≤ 2β2s

< 2H , H = ( β + 1)2 s+1 .

The bound H has O(s + log( β + 1)) bits. Evaluation of every gate modulo an arbitrary binary integer m is polynomial time, and supplies the representative required by Lemma 15. We obtain PosSLP ∈ PNP PP 

. This also covers the usual formulation with constants 0 and 1, since a binary integer constant can be constructed with a number of gates linear in its encoding length. For coefficient positivity, let f be an integer polynomial SLP with a target exponent vector supplied in binary. Its total degree is at most 2 s. Its coefficient ℓ1-norm obeys the same recurrence as the magnitude bound above: addition and subtraction are subadditive, multiplication is submultiplicative, and an input variable has norm 1. Every coefficient therefore satisfies the same bound |c| < 2H . We immediately return zero if a target exponent exceeds 2 s. Otherwise Lemma 14 gives uniform # P representatives for c = [ Xk]f

modulo every short integer. These meet the hypothesis of Lemma 15, proving coefficient positivity in PNP PP 

as well. Neither application requires writing the potentially exponential-length integer whose sign is being tested. The representation hypothesis also describes the limit of this result for the ETR construction. To invoke the sign criterion there, one would need GapP PH representatives of its scalar norm expression, or a reduction to a coefficient of a polynomial-size SLP. The available indexed norm computations have not been put in that form. For example, a modular product algorithm may first retrieve a counting value and then exponentiate a field element by that value. The resulting polynomial-time computation with a counting oracle is not one of the sums or fixed products covered by GapP closure. The coefficient lemma handles an SLP coefficient directly, but does not eliminate that separate nonlinear operation. 

# I Short-seed randomized sign reconstruction 

The first-event argument locates a useful scale by asking whether an earlier event exists. We can replace this existential search by a randomized magnitude test. The test multiplies the unknown displacement by a random integer and observes the resulting phase. A multiplier with exponentially many bits is useful here, so we generate its bits from a short seed and evaluate the phase without constructing the multiplier. This gives a second upper bound for the integer problems. 

Theorem 9. The problems PosSLP and positivity of a specified coefficient of an integer polynomial SLP belong to BPP PP ⊆ C2P.

We first state the sign-reconstruction interface. All length bounds in the next lemma refer to the original input length n; in particular, the indices of exponential sums have polynomial length. 

Lemma 16. Suppose 

S(x) = ∑

> i<L

A(x, i )

m(x, i ) = z + δ, z ∈ Z,

where L, J are positive integers with polynomial-length binary descriptions computable in polynomial time from x, the positive integers m(x, i ) are polynomial-bit and polynomial-time computable, and A(x, i ) is a uniform GapP B function satisfying |A(x, i )| ≤ 2a(n) for a known polynomial a. Assume 

δ = 0 or 2−J ≤ | δ| < 2−32 .

If the relevant GapP B functions can be retrieved exactly in FP D , then the sign of δ is decidable in BPP D .

The retrieval hypothesis applies to the complete weighted sums formed below, rather than only to individual numerators. For our integer application we use B = PH , D = PP , and exact functional counting absorption. For the norm construction we use B = C3P and D = C4P.

37 I.1 Short seeds for long phase products 

A Fourier shape on T bits is a function

We need a generator which fools every such product and allows direct access to an output coordinate. The following recursive construction uses standard small-bias ideas [5, 6]. We give the proof and a field implementation to make the required random access explicit. 

Lemma 17. Given T ≥ 1 in binary and an integer q ≥ 2, put ϵ = 2 −q . There is a uniform generator

Consequently 

ϵr+1 ≤ 2ϵr + λ, ϵ0 = 0 , ϵk ≤ (2 k − 1) λ < ϵ. 

Discarding padded coordinates preserves this guarantee, since their Fourier factors can be chosen identically one. We can choose each field degree er to be the least power of two at least ⌈log 2(dr /λ )⌉. Then dr+1 = dr +2 er ,giving dk = O(log 2(T /ϵ )). A requested output coordinate follows only one branch at every level of (40). For a right branch, compute the short vector Vr(a, b ) and change the seed; for a left branch, retain the seed. There are only k such steps. Here the field presentations can also be constructed deterministically within the claimed time. Start with 

K0 = F2, γ0 = 1, and recursively set 

Kr+1 = Kr [θ]/(θ2 + θ + γr ), γr+1 = γr θ. 

Inductively γr has absolute trace one. The quadratic is therefore irreducible by the Artin–Schreier criterion, and its relative trace sends θ to one. Transitivity of trace shows that γr+1 again has absolute trace one. This constructs the required power-of-two degrees. Arithmetic in the quadratic tower, and trace by iterated Frobenius squaring, take polynomial time in the final field degree. The coordinate algorithm is therefore polynomial-time, without any field-search oracle.

# I.3 Finding a scale with bounded-error tests 

For a genuinely uniform t-bit integer K, define 

μt = E0≤K< 2t cos(2 πKδ ).

The following estimates separate small and large scales: 2t|δ| ≤ 1/16 =⇒ μt > 9/10 ,

2t|δ| ≥ 4 =⇒ |μt| ≤ 1/8. (43) The first follows because every phase has absolute value less than π/ 8. For the second, sum a geometric progression and use |sin( πδ )| ≥ 2|δ|:

∣∣EK e2πiKδ ∣∣ =

Generate the multiplier bits using Lemma 17 with error 1 /128. The resulting expectation differs from μt

by at most 1 /128, since the complex exponential is a Fourier shape. The preceding phase evaluation adds 

39 less than 1 /512. Independent samples estimate their expectation within 1 /64 with failure probability ρ, using 

O(log(1 /ρ )) seeds by Hoeffding’s inequality. Hence we obtain an estimate ̂μ t with 

|̂μ t − μt| < 1/16 except with probability ρ. Every sample is an FP D computation on polynomial-length inputs. Call a tested scale high when ̂μ t > 1/2, and low otherwise. On an accurate test, high at t =⇒ 2t|δ| < 4, low at t =⇒ 2t|δ| > 1/16 . (44) Labels inside the transition range need not be monotone. We use only these two implications. 

Proof of Lemma 16. First test tmax = J + 3. If δ̸ = 0, then 2 tmax |δ| ≥ 8, so an accurate test is low. If δ = 0, every generated phase is zero modulo integers and the test is high. A high answer therefore gives the zero output, subject to the stated error probability. Otherwise start with a high label at t = 0, where K = 0, and the low label at tmax . Bisect this interval, retaining a high left endpoint and a low right endpoint according to each tested midpoint. After O(log J)tests the endpoints are adjacent: h is high and h + 1 is low. On accurate tests, (44) gives 1/32 < 2h|δ| < 4.

This conclusion uses only the labels of the two endpoints, so arbitrary behavior within the transition range causes no problem. Since |δ| < 2−32 , the low endpoint is at least 29 and h ≥ 28. Set j = h − 6. Then 1/2048 < |2j δ| < 1/16 . (45) One deterministic phase query now determines the sign. Take b0 = a(n) + ⌈log 2 L⌉ + 30 and retrieve

Its normalized centered remainder is within 2 −30 of 2 j δ. Equation (45) ensures that its strict sign equals that of δ.There is a known polynomial bound Q = O(log( J + 4)) on the number of tests. Give each test failure probability at most 1 /(10 Q) and use fresh seeds. Conditional on any preceding transcript, the next scale is fixed and its error bound still holds. A union bound gives success probability at least 9 /10 for the entire adaptive search. All seeds and indices have polynomial length, establishing BPP D . The error can be reduced to 2 − poly( n) by standard repetition.

# J Intermediate CH bounds and the ordered-rank refinement 

We retain the geometric reduction of Section G and improve the implementation of its final sign test. For every polynomial-length parameter choice ω, that reduction constructs a nonzero integer 

Zω = Norm A

(U (ℓ)), |Zω | < 2H , H = 2 poly( n).

Every negative value certifies feasibility, and a feasible instance has a choice with negative value. The polynomial-size sign-filter circuits and short Thom conjunction remain unchanged. The improvement comes from evaluating their nested norms modulo short integers, rather than recovering intermediate coefficient arrays. An earlier implementation used full Thom-code hashing and the general resultant circuits of [10]. Its conservative arithmetic-depth ledger gave C1023 P, recorded in the supplementary treatment. That ledger is not needed below. Here we account directly for the specialized computation of Section G, obtaining the intermediate bounds C7P, C6P, and C5P, followed by the C4P ordered-rank refinement. Throughout this section, all indices and finite-ring elements have polynomial bit length. Bounds on degrees and integer bit lengths may have singly exponential values, but have polynomial-length binary descriptions. One fixed polynomial bounds these lengths and the running times uniformly over every permitted ω and every supplied modulus. We use C0P = P and Ch+1 P = PP ChP.

# J.1 A four-level residue computation 

Write the critical equations as Gi(X) = X5

If p > 5, all initial Jacobian entries are units. Normalizing by that Jacobian and applying Lemma 13 gives polynomial-size polynomial SLPs Bb(t) approximating the formal branches, indexed by b ∈ { 1, . . . , 5}m. The coefficients are computed modulo p; we specialize at t = 1. We use the same branches for the inner norm defining qt, its selected derivative evaluations, the cleared selector, and its outer norm. Polynomial operations, including coefficient extraction and substitution, preserve agreement modulo tK . Consequently there is no need to extract homotopy coefficients between the inner and outer norms. Here the required precision can be fixed before choosing the field. If an element V (t, X ) has t-degree L

and X-degree E, its multiplication matrix in the standard monomial basis satisfies deg t Norm( V ) ≤ D(L + E + 4 m).

41 Indeed, monic reduction starts in total X-degree at most E + 4 m; each reduction decreases this degree and introduces at most one power of t. Applying this bound to the nested construction gives a uniform

The exponent is positive, and only the desired coefficient contributes a power whose exponent is a positive multiple of p − 1. Several auxiliary extraction variables can be indexed together in one counting sum. An exponential product of polynomial-time computable nonzero field elements lies in FP #P. Choose a primitive root by FP NP preprocessing, and count the sum of the factors’ unique discrete logarithms. Explicitly, for factors ui and generator g, count triples ( i, e, j ) satisfying ge = ui and 0 ≤ j < e < p − 1. Exponentiating 

g by that count gives the product. Zero factors are handled separately. The preprocessing is performed before the ordinary counting queries, with the generator passed as a parameter. Relative to ChP, this costs one further counting level. The distinction between a short value and its counting representation is important here. An exponential sum of short nonnegative values computable in FP ChP is a # PChP function: guess the summand index and an integer below its value. The answer still has polynomially many bits, and polynomially many threshold queries retrieve it in FP Ch+1 P. We charge this retrieval whenever its answer will be evaluated inside a subsequent counting computation. This explains the separate levels for products and coefficient extraction. The resulting ledger concerns entire short field values: operation function class one branch factor FP

The Hasse derivatives in our construction are extracted directly from q(z + v). The final row combines all coefficients through KZ in one Fourier sum with a geometric-series weight. Binary doubling evaluates that weight in polynomial time. Polynomially many adaptive calls to an existing function oracle do not add a level. To recover the sign, choose an interval of polynomial-bit primes whose product M exceeds 8 |Zω |.Elementary prime-product bounds give such an interval above the required degree cutoff, together with 

M < 2H0 for a polynomial-bit H0. Index all integers in the interval and give composites zero weight. The CRT cofactors require only an FP C1P product, so the scalar residues give weights Ai ∈ FP C4P satisfying

Concretely, at scale j the lemma forms a polynomial-precision approximation to the fractional part of 2 j Zω /M . The scale index can be exponentially large because 2 j mod pi is computed from its binary description. Before the first approximation whose circular distance from an integer is at least 1 /8, the true signed distance has magnitude below 1 /8 + 2 −9 < 1/4. Doubling cannot cross the opposite half of the circle. The first departure therefore reveals the original sign; the height bound guarantees that it occurs within the allowed scales. Guessing the first event and universally excluding earlier events gives a Σ C5P

# J.3 Retaining the last Fourier sum 

Let T be the Teichmüller unit group. Orthogonality gives

> KZ

Here both the degree and extraction-index restrictions are covered by our choice of Q. The geometric weight is evaluated by binary doubling, without division by ξ − 1. Take the canonical constant coordinate of each whole summand and sum these nonnegative integers without reducing the total . This gives Wpa ∈ #PC3P

with Wpa ≡ Zω (mod pa). The total ring element is scalar because the starting grid disappears at t = 1; intermediate coefficients need not be scalar. We use a coordinate projection, not a field trace. This is where the implementation differs from the seven-level route. Computing the canonical residue of the last sum would give an FP C4P function. Instead, we leave its accepting-path count intact. Its numerical value may exceed pa, but every later use needs only its congruence modulo pa. This permits the final reconstruction to combine counting paths directly, without invoking an additional oracle that retrieves that count. Ordinary short CRT weights combine the polynomially many prime-power representatives into 

W (M ) ∈ GapP C3P, W (M ) ≡ Zω (mod M ).

Its output has polynomial bit length: it is an exponential sum of short coordinates with short weights. Factorization and consistent ring selection are available inside the same oracle bound.

Guessing the first event, its negative side, and ω, followed by universally excluding earlier events, yields Σ C4P 

> 2

. Finally, ΣC4P 

> 2

⊆ PH C4P ⊆ PPP C4P

= PC5P ⊆ C6P,

by relativized Toda [13] and deterministic simulation inside PP . This proves the decision containment. The nested norm representatives remain in GapP C3P, rather than GapP PH ; the argument does not place ∃R in 

PH PP .

44 J.4 A fifth-level bound from randomized reconstruction 

The preceding C6P proof remains valid independently of the randomized reconstruction lemma. We now improve its final step while retaining the same geometric certificate and modular computation. Randomized sign recovery removes the existential search over scales; the remaining existential choice is the original ETR certificate. 

Theorem 10. We have 

∃R ⊆ MA C4P ⊆ C5P.

Proof. Let x have length n, and pad the certificate ω from Section G to a fixed polynomial length m(n). Invalid encodings are rejected. For every valid certificate, the construction gives a nonzero integer Zx,ω with 

|Zx,ω | < 2H(n), and 

x is feasible ⇐⇒ ∃ω Z x,ω < 0,

where H(n) = 2 P (n) for a fixed polynomial P . This height bound is uniform over all certificates. Soundness includes inaccurate analytic choices: a negative real selector factor always supplies a qualifying real critical point, whereas nonreal factors have positive conjugate products. Generic choices are needed only for completeness. The modular ledger is unchanged. The inner norm has values in FP C1P, the selected Hasse derivatives and cleared selector in FP C2P, and the outer norm at a short ring point in FP C3P. Leaving its last Fourier sum unreduced gives 

W (x, ω, M ) ∈ GapP C3P, W (x, ω, M ) ≡ Zx,ω (mod M )for every positive polynomial-bit modulus M . The output-bit bound is uniform in n + |ω| + log M . All prime powers, including small characteristics, are covered by the unit-grid construction above. Set N = H + 16 and Mi = N + 1 + i, for 0 ≤ i ≤ N . Lemma 14 supplies polynomial-bit Bi ∈ GapP NP 

with Bi ≡ (Ni

) (mod Mi). The beta identity gives 

S =

> N

∑

> i=0

(−1) iBiW (x, ω, M i)

Mi

= z + δ, δ = CN Zx,ω , CN = (N !) 2

(2 N + 1)! > 0.

Since CN ≤ 4−N , we have |δ| < 2−32 . Since Zx,ω is a nonzero integer, 

|δ| ≥ CN ≥ 2−J , J = (2 N + 1) ⌈log 2(2 N + 1) ⌉.

Both N and J have polynomial-length binary descriptions. The larger choice of N supplies the small initial displacement required by Lemma 16. The signed numerators are in GapP C3P, because NP ⊆ C3P and only a product of two counting functions is involved. Every phase query in Lemma 16 is an exponentially indexed linear combination of these numerators with polynomial-time computable short weights. Even its double index, consisting of a denominator index and a multiplier-bit index, has polynomial length. The whole query therefore remains in GapP C3P and is exactly retrievable in FP PP C3P

= FP C4P. This verifies the lemma’s retrieval hypothesis for the complete weighted sums, not merely for their individual summands. We obtain 

{(x, ω ) : Zx,ω < 0} ∈ BPP C4P.

Merlin now sends ω, and Arthur runs this sign algorithm with fresh random bits. A feasible instance has one fixed witness accepted with probability at least 2 /3. On an infeasible instance, every witness is accepted with probability at most 1 /3. The pointwise guarantee of Lemma 16 is sufficient: the witness is fixed before Arthur’s randomness, and no seed must work simultaneously for all witnesses. This proves membership in 

MA C4P.We spell out the last containment to avoid an extra hierarchy level. Fix a complete oracle A for C4P,amplify Arthur’s verifier to error ε ≤ 2−m(n)−4, and pad every computation to use exactly r(n) ≥ 1 random bits. The function 

F (x) = # {(ω, ρ ) : |ω| = m(n), |ρ| = r(n), V A(x, ω ; ρ) = 1 }

45 belongs to # PA. On a yes instance, F (x) ≥ (1 − ε)2 r(n) > 2r(n)−1. On a no instance, 

F (x) ≤ 2m(n)ε2r(n) ≤ 2r(n)−4 < 2r(n)−1.

Thus the sign of the GapP A function F (x)−2r(n)−1 decides the language. Consequently MA C4P ⊆ PP C4P = C5P.The amplification is essential: averaging a constant-error verifier over all witnesses would not give this gap. The verifier used in this refinement has bounded error. The final C5P containment uses an unbounded-error PP computation with a C4P oracle to count its amplified accepting computations. The inner norm, derivative, and outer norm layers have not been flattened, and the conclusion remains a decision containment. 

# J.5 Ordered ranks instead of high derivatives 

We can remove the derivative-extraction layer by giving every real root a short integer label. The label need not be its usual rank among the real parts of all complex roots. It is enough that the labels increase strictly along the real roots. This distinction permits a short rational construction even when a projected difference is extremely close to the imaginary axis. 

Theorem 11. 

∃R ⊆ BPP C3P ⊆ C4P.

We first prove the analytic ordering property, then implement the labels with first-order jets. Finally, random hashing replaces the guessed isolating list. 

A complex normalizer. Let a finite set of M nonzero complex numbers satisfy 2 −L ≤ | z| ≤ 2L, where log L and log M are polynomially bounded. Put 

T0 = ⌈10 (log 2(L + 2) + log 2(M + 2) + 10 )⌉ , C = 2 B ≥ 10 6M (T0 + 2) .

Here and below the least sufficient power of two may be used. For positive s, define

C−1 min {s|z|, (s|z|)−1} ≤ | ϕs(z)| ≤ 1.

Start with A = 1, giving the lower exponent K = L + B + 1. Thereafter take A = 2 ⌈K/ 2⌉ and replace K by 

⌈K/ 2⌉ + B + 1. After polynomially many stages, at most T0, all magnitudes lie in [2 −K , 1] with K ≤ 2B + 4. Conditional on all previous stages being good, the next bad-event estimate applies to every current value. A union bound therefore gives simultaneous success with probability greater than 0 .999. at every specified nonzero input. The function has real coefficients, R(0) = 0, and positive denominator on the real line. Every seed preserves the sign of every nonzero real input. All scale choices have polynomially many bits; the possibly large powers 2 ⌈K/ 2⌉ have short powering circuits. The resulting integer-pair SLP has polynomial size and singly exponential degree and coefficient bit height. The angular step can change a complex input’s half-plane. The following property is what the rank construction actually needs.

Every substitution and norm multiplies singly exponential degree and height bounds by at most another singly exponential factor. The number of such layers is fixed. Thus the same shared-homotopy truncation and actual-degree Fourier bounds apply uniformly to the entire construction. Repeat 200( ℓ + 1) independent trials, amplifying each sign test to error at most 1 /[100 · 200( ℓ + 1)], and accept if any test reports a negative norm. On a feasible instance the probability of never isolating a root is at most e−10 . On either kind of instance, the probability that any sign test errs is at most 1 /100. This proves ∃R ⊆ BPP C3P. Finally relativized BPP ⊆ PP gives BPP C3P ⊆ PP C3P = C4P. The sign subroutine has two-sided error, so this argument does not claim an RP C3P containment. 

# K Exact rational weighted counting 

The sign-reconstruction lemmas also apply to a class defined independently of arithmetic circuits. Following de Campos, Stamoulis, and Weyland [4, Definition 9], let PP (pt )

# K.1 Two factor promises for scalar circuits 

Two restricted families admit substantially lower bounds. These are promises on the output integer, not properties established for all SLPs or for the norm in the ETR reduction. 

Proposition 2. Let Z be the output of an integer SLP. If every maximal prime-power divisor of |Z|, when nonzero, is at most 2B , where B is supplied in unary, its sign is decidable in ⊕P. If instead a positive binary integer M is supplied with the promise that every prime factor of nonzero |Z| divides M , its sign is decidable in deterministic polynomial time. 

51 Proof. For the first promise, evaluation modulo 2 B+1 detects zero and otherwise determines v = v2(|Z|). Evaluation modulo 2 B+2 then gives r = ( Z/ 2v ) mod 4, which is either 1 or 3. The # P count 

h = # {(p, k ) : p prime , k ≥ 1, p ≡ 3 (mod 4) , p k ≤ 2B , p k | Z}

counts the multiplicities of all prime factors congruent to 3 modulo 4. The positive odd part of |Z| is consequently ( −1) h mod 4. Thus Z > 0 exactly when r = ( −1) h mod 4. A parity machine tests this equality by adding a known zero or one to h. Every prime-power divisibility test uses a polynomial-bit modulus. For the second promise, put q = 8 M − 1 and compute the Jacobi symbol 

( Zq

)

using the short residue 

Z mod q. The integer q need not be prime. For every odd prime p | M , quadratic reciprocity and q ≡ − 1(mod p) give ( pq

)

=

( qp

)

(−1) (p−1) /2 = 1 .

Also 

( 2

> q

)

= 1 and 

( −1

> q

)

= −1. By multiplicativity, 

( Zq

)

= sgn Z on nonzero promised inputs. Such inputs are coprime to q, so the zero case is detected as well. Modular SLP evaluation and the short Jacobi computation are both in polynomial time. The second promise permits arbitrarily large prime-power exponents. For example, if every prime factor is at most a supplied unary bound B, use the polynomial-bit integer M = B!. 

# L Ideal membership and conditional square certificates 

The preceding construction uses two nested products over formal roots. We next consider approaches that do not begin by optimizing those products. They give two additional restricted results and a precise certificate problem for a stronger real-feasibility bound. They do not improve the general containments in Theorems 1 and 5. 

# L.1 Exact membership in a finite monic algebra 

Consider integer polynomial circuits describing

Conditional on success, the selected prime is uniform over the eligible primes. If c̸ = 0, it has at most H distinct prime divisors, so the chance that p | c is less than 1 /16. # L.2 Real emptiness and four-square certificates 

There is a particularly small extension of the critical-point reduction. Use the fixed weighted objective 

J = Jw, and adjoin 

Y 8 + J(X) − T = 0 . (1) Its tail has total degree at most six. Together with the degree-five Gi, this is again a system of the form in Theorem 13, of dimension 8 · 5m. A real solution of (1) exists over a real critical point exactly when J(X) ≤ T .Feasible instances have a critical minimum strictly below T ; infeasible instances have J(X) > T at every real point. Thus the original ETR instance is feasible exactly when this finite rational algebra B has a real point. Neither genericity nor reducedness is needed. The large scalar Q retains its short-SLP representation. 

Lemma 22. A nonzero finite-dimensional commutative rational algebra B has no real points if and only if 

−1 is a sum of four squares in B.Proof. The reduced quotient is a product of number fields. If there are no real points, none of these fields has a real embedding. The form ⟨1, 1, 1, 1, 1⟩ is isotropic over every completion: the archimedean completions are complex, and quadratic forms of dimension at least five over nonarchimedean local number fields are isotropic. Hasse–Minkowski therefore gives a nontrivial zero over each number field [18]. Dividing by one nonzero coordinate writes −1 as four squares. Assemble these identities in the product of the fields. Lift the four elements arbitrarily to B, obtaining ∑

c2 + U 21 + U 22 + U 23 + U 24 ∈ (G1, . . . , G m, Y 8 + J − T ), c ∈ Z \ { 0}. (2) If the Ui and c are supplied as short integer circuits, Theorem 13 checks the identity in coRP PP . Nonzeroness of c has a short additional witness: an integer modulus q > 1 with c mod q̸ = 0. The circuit height bound and the prime-counting argument above ensure that a polynomial-bit such modulus exists. 

Proposition 3 (Conditional certificate bound) . Suppose every real-empty algebra obtained from (1) admits certificates (2) whose total integer-SLP size is bounded by a fixed polynomial in the original input length. Then 

∃R ⊆ coMA PP ⊆ C2P ∩ PH PP .

54 Proof. Merlin supplies the circuits and the nonzero-modulus witness. The preceding verifier has perfect completeness and bounded soundness error. This puts infeasibility in MA PP . The usual inclusions MA ⊆ PP 

and MA ⊆ ΣP 

> 2

relativize; take complements. The circuit-size hypothesis is open here. Four elements can still require exponentially many coordinates in the standard basis. The short Newton iteration in Lemma 22 only lifts representatives that have already been found. There is a direct variant in the original critical algebra A. Keep the fixed threshold and put h = J − T .On an infeasible instance, h is positive at every real point. In each residue number field, a nonzero such element is four squares: apply Hasse–Minkowski to ⟨1, 1, 1, 1, −h⟩. If the last coordinate of an isotropic vector is nonzero, division gives the representation; otherwise ⟨1, 1, 1, 1⟩ is isotropic and contains a hyperbolic plane, so it represents every field element. On a component where h = 0, use four zeros. Consequently there are integer polynomial representatives satisfying

Theorem 13 verifies a short supplied identity. At a feasible critical minimum its left side evaluates to a strictly negative number, proving soundness. Thus a polynomial circuit-size bound for these direct certificates gives the same conditional coMA PP containment, with no extra variable, unit condition, or nilpotent lifting. Short representatives in the reduced algebra are still unproved. Even fixed algebra dimension does not settle this compression problem. For an integer SLP value a,consider Ba = Q[x]/(x2 + a). It is real-empty exactly when a > 0. If all these algebras admitted polynomial-size certificates (2), then PosSLP would be in MA ∩ coMA . Indeed, reduce each certificate circuit modulo 

x2 + a, representing its value by a pair of scalar SLPs. The two required scalar identities are testable by the ordinary coRP zero test for integer SLPs. A nonzero modulus witnesses c̸ = 0. This gives PosSLP ∈ MA ; apply the same argument to −a and include the coRP zero test to obtain its complement in MA . This implication is not a lower bound against short certificates. It explains why fixed-degree number-field existence theorems do not supply the missing complexity bound. A related sufficient hypothesis was studied explicitly by Saranurak and Jindal [19]: whether every positive integer a has an addition-multiplication chain of length τ+(a) ≤ (1 + τ (a)) O(1) , where τ also permits subtraction. Their Claim 2 guesses such a chain and checks equality by the integer-SLP zero test, obtaining a conditional Σ P 

> 2

bound. The same verifier is an MA protocol; handling −a and zero gives MA ∩ coMA . No polynomial domination is proved here. More general arithmetic circuits for semialgebraic certificates are studied in the cone proof system of Alekseev et al. [20]; their efficient verification likewise does not assert polynomial-size certificates for all inputs. 

# L.3 A square-class promise for integer signs 

There is also a useful strengthening of the earlier factor-support promise. Large primes can be allowed when their exponents are even. 

Theorem 14. Let Z be an integer SLP value and let M > 0 be a supplied polynomial-bit integer. Promise that either Z = 0 , or 

vp(|Z|) odd =⇒ p | M for every prime p. 

Then deciding Z > 0 is in RP . If Z̸ = 0 is also promised, its sign is computable in expected polynomial time with zero error. 

The nonzero values have the form Z = εdu 2, with ε ∈ {− 1, 1} and d a squarefree divisor of rad( M ). Neither d nor u is supplied. For q = 8 M t − 1, quadratic reciprocity gives 

( Zq

)

= ε whenever gcd( Z, q ) = 1 . (3) 

55 Indeed, q ≡ 7 (mod 8), so (2 /q ) = 1 and ( −1/q ) = −1. For every odd p | d, one has q ≡ − 1 (mod p), and the two reciprocity signs cancel to give ( p/q ) = 1. The square factor contributes one. If the gcd is not one, the Jacobi symbol is zero. Every nonzero answer is therefore correct. For completeness, a short sampling interval suffices to find a nonzero answer. Given 0 < |Z| < 2H , put 

h = ⌈log 2(H + 2) ⌉, b = 2 h + 64 , z = 2 b,m0 = 40(1 + ⌈log 2 b⌉) + 1 , T = z2m0+10 .

For any positive even integer a, a uniform t ∈ { 1, . . . , T } satisfies Pr[gcd( at − 1, Z ) = 1] ≥ 1128 b3 . (4) Here log T = O(log H log log( H + 2)), so t is short. To prove (4), let P contain the prime divisors p ≤ z of Z with p ∤ a. They are all at least three. The elementary central-binomial estimate on dyadic intervals gives

Apply (4) with a = 8 M . Modular circuit evaluation and the Euclidean Jacobi algorithm take polynomial time. Accept positivity if any of 256 b3 independent trials yields +1. A nonpositive value is never accepted, while a positive value is missed with probability at most e−2. Under the nonzero promise, repeat until the symbol is nonzero; the expected number of trials is at most 128 b3. This proves Theorem 14. The representation of M is essential. Here the denominator has a short SLP, but can have exponentially many bits. Evaluating a Jacobi symbol with such a succinct denominator already contains the original sign problem. 

# M Deterministic parameters and square-class promises 

The preceding general containments have not decreased further. This section records two ways to simplify their arithmetic, a stronger promise result for integer circuits, and a broader certificate target. The distinction between a short description and a small dense degree bound remains essential. 

# M.1 Direct arithmetic at the prime two 

The proof of Lemma 9 does more than remove generic choices. At any ordinary polynomial precision a, all the critical points can be evaluated directly in the fixed-rank Galois ring GR(2 a, 6). An index in {1, . . . , 5}m

These observations apply to a modified version of the rational normalizer. # M.2 A small squarefree kernel need not be supplied 

Theorem 15. Let an integer SLP compute A, with |A| < 2H , and let B ≥ 1 be supplied in unary. Promise that either A = 0 , or 

A = ku 2, u > 0, k signed squarefree , |k| < 2B .

h = ⌈log 2(H + 2) ⌉, b = 16( h + B + 12) , z = 2 b, s = 40(1 + ⌈log 2 b⌉) + 1 , T = z2s+10 .

For a nonsquare nonzero A, the Jacobi character χ(q) = ( k/q ) is nonprincipal on the units modulo C = 8 |k|.For negative k, its value at C − 1 is −1. For positive squarefree k > 1, quadratic reciprocity and CRT give a nonresidue class; the case k = 2 uses q = 5 (mod 8). Exactly φ(C)/2 unit classes therefore have value −1. > 1≤q≤T

[q odd , (A/q ) = −1] ≥ 1512 b3 . (53) All sampled q have polynomially many bits. Evaluate A mod q and its Jacobi symbol in polynomial time. Repeating 1024 b3 times rejects every nonsquare with probability greater than 2 /3; a square, including zero, is never rejected. For positivity Merlin guesses 1 ≤ d < 2B and a modulus 2 ≤ r ≤ 2( H + 1) at which A is nonzero. Arthur checks that remainder and applies the square test to Ad . Every guessed d, including a false one, satisfies 

| kernel( Ad )| < 22B , |Ad | < 2H+B .

If A > 0, choosing d = k gives a square and perfect acceptance. If A < 0, every Ad is a nonsquare. Zero is excluded by the modulus witness. The complement has the analogous protocol for −A, together with a zero tag checked by the usual coRP integer-circuit identity test. This proves the MA claims. For recovery, use the parameters of the square test with B′ = 2 B and H′ = H + B, and denote the resulting values by b′, T ′. A common sample of 1024( b′)3(B + 2) moduli eliminates all false pairs ( σ, d ), 

σ ∈ {− 1, 1}, 1 ≤ d < 2B , with probability at least 3 /4, by amplification and a union bound over fewer than 2B+1 pairs. True pairs always survive. NP prefix queries find a surviving pair with smallest d. A further NP query asks whether any odd 1 < q ≤ T ′ has ( σAd/q ) = −1; by (53) this detects every false pair exactly. Restart if it does. Otherwise minimality implies d = |k|, since all true pairs have d = |k|v2. The expected number of rounds is at most 4 /3. Zero can be decided by one preliminary NP query for 2 ≤ r ≤ 2( H + 1) with A mod r̸ = 0. Such a modulus exists for nonzero A, since lcm(1 , . . . , 2( H + 1)) ≥ 2H+1 . Finally, each bit of the unique signed kernel has an MA certificate: for nonzero A, guess ( σ, d ), certify the squarefreeness of d by its short sorted prime factorization, supply a nonzero modulus for A, check the claimed bit, and use the same square test. Use a sign bit and B magnitude bits, with zero encoded by all zeros. A zero tag is permitted only when claiming bit zero and is checked by the ordinary coRP zero test. The opposite bit has the same certificate format. Thus each bit lies in PP and the kernel is recoverable in FP PP .The promise has not been proved for unrestricted circuit outputs or for the norms in the ETR construction. A fixed list of Jacobi moduli can be hidden by a large square factor: A = ±(∏ 

> j

qj )2 gives zero residues at all the listed qj . This is a limitation of such a preselected list, not a uniform lower bound against deterministic algorithms that inspect the circuit. 

# M.3 Allowing weighted and coefficient-defined certificates 

The four-square target in Section L can be relaxed. Permit a polynomial-time generator, on a polynomial-length index i, to specify coefficient-defined integer polynomials Pi and scalar weights wi. A certificate asserts 

c2(J − T ) − ∑

> i

wiP 2 

> i

∈ √I, c̸ = 0 , wi ≥ 0.

All circuits, coefficient indices, and the generator must be fixed before verifier randomness; dependence on the sampled prime would not certify a rational identity. For the radical identity, expand ∑ 

> α

Tr( XαF )2 with F = c2(J − T ) − ∑ 

> i

wiP 2 

> i

. There are only two square indices in each summand. Four independent copies of the coefficient variables represent the two squared polynomials; the tensor residue formula adds the trace factors. The resulting coefficient sums have one raw modular counting representation, so their zero test remains in coRP PP .The weights require a separate sign check. Amplify the BPP PP coefficient-sign algorithm enough that a union bound covers every index, using one shared random tape. For a fixed tape, existence of a weight declared negative is an NP PP predicate. Consequently a supplied certificate can be verified in BPP NP PP 

. If every NO instance had such a certificate of polynomial description size, this would give 

∃R ⊆ coMA NP PP

The effective rational finite-set Positivstellensatz of Baldi, Krick and Mourrain [21] supplies dense degree and height bounds, but its Gram matrix construction, rounding, and factorization have dimension polynomial in the full quotient dimension. They do not provide the short coefficient generator required here. 

# N Fixed scalar denominators for sums of squares 

The certificate approach leaves two different representation questions: how to describe a common scalar denominator, and how to describe the polynomials being squared. We can settle the first question without solving the second. In every reduced finite integral algebra, an element positive at all real points has a sum-of-squares representation with a prescribed power of two as denominator. Its exponent is polynomial in the dense algebra parameters, hence has polynomial binary length in our critical-point construction. The number of squares and their dense coefficient heights have the same type of bound. An odd-denominator refinement uses the special reduction modulo two of the critical algebra. These are existence and height statements. They do not provide short circuits for the numerators. We make that distinction explicit before applying the coefficient-zero verifier. 

# N.1 A quantitative dyadic representation

# N.2 An odd denominator when the algebra is étale at two 

In the critical algebra, the Gram map has an integral right inverse. This lets us replace every dyadic grid by a triadic one and keep the denominator odd.

# N.3 What is short in the critical-point application 

The exponent L is genuinely short in binary for the algebras used here. For fixed d, suppose Gi = Xdi + ri

with deg ri < d . Let s0 bound the number of monomials in each ri, and let their coefficient bit heights be at most H. A product of two standard basis monomials has total degree at most 2 m(d − 1). Every reduction step decreases total degree, so the structure constants admit the crude bound 

B ≤ (max(2 , s 0)2 H )2m(d−1)+1 .

If an observable has degree e, at most t0 monomials, and coefficient bit heights at most Hh, its normal-form coefficients are bounded by 

C ≤ max(2 , t 0)2 Hh

(max(2 , s 0)2 H )e+1 .

These estimates just count reduction branches and multiply their coefficient bounds. We use the logarithms of their right sides in (54); there is no need to write the integers B and C explicitly. For our weighted critical system, d = 5, D = 5 m, and h = J − T has degree six. All logarithmic bounds above have singly exponential values and polynomial binary length. Consequently L is computable in binary in polynomial time, and 2 L and 3 L have polynomial-size integer SLPs. On every infeasible instance, Theorem 16 gives at most exponentially many integral numerators, of standard degree at most 4 m, with singly exponential coefficient bit heights. The scalar is fixed by the input bounds, rather than supplied as an unknown part of the witness. What is missing is a coherent short description of those numerators. A dense algorithm polynomial in 

D does not give polynomial-time access in log D to its output coefficients. In particular, the explicit linear section of the Gram map does not make evaluation of the real CRT matrix, matrix square roots, rounding, or integer four-square decompositions a succinct computation. 

# N.4 Verifying a compressed family of squares 

We can allow a more flexible description than an ordinary SLP for each numerator. An integer polynomial 

Pi(X) is coefficient-defined if a short integer polynomial circuit Fi(X, Y ) and a binary exponent vector αi

specify 

Pi(X) = [ Y αi ]Fi(X, Y ).

A family is coherent if one polynomial-size description generates these data in polynomial time from the polynomial-length index i. The runtime and program sizes have fixed polynomial bounds in the original input and certificate lengths. The syntactic degrees and coefficient bit heights have singly exponential upper bounds with polynomial-length binary descriptions. The generated integer circuits are fixed before verifier randomness; they cannot depend on the sampled prime. 

Proposition 5. In a finite pure-monic algebra of Theorem 13, a supplied coherent coefficient-defined family permits a coRP PP test of 

c2h = ∑

The expression on the right is a nonnegative integer. Expand the square before retrieving any modular values. Only two independent square indices occur in each term. At most four independent copies of the coefficient variables represent the corresponding squared polynomials. The two residue factors have the finite polynomial coefficient formula used in the proof of Theorem 13. For a prime larger than every resulting individual degree, finite-field orthogonality expands all these extractions into one sum of polynomial-time computable residue weights. Its indices consist of the two family indices, the basis index, and the coefficient-extraction points; together they have polynomial binary length. The signed sum has one raw GapP representative, or one can sum nonnegative residues in # P. A PP oracle retrieves its residue. No canonical counting output is substituted into a later counting summand. The integer U (F ) has a singly exponential bit-height bound. The prime sampler and divisibility argument of Lemma 21 therefore give a one-sided zero test. A true identity always passes; a false identity fails except with bounded probability. Allowing coefficient-defined numerators makes the verifier more permissive; it does not turn a large table of coefficients into a short description. 

# N.5 Why a positive moment functional is a different target 

A rational positive functional cannot replace the Gram certificate in general. If a reduced rational algebra is a product of number fields, every rational linear functional has the form ℓ(a) = Tr( ca ). At a complex embedding with c = u + iv , its quadratic form on squares contributes 2 Re ((u + iv )( x + iy )2) = 2 u(x2 − y2) − 4vxy. 

The eigenvalues of this block are ±2|u + iv |. Nonnegativity on rational vectors implies nonnegativity on the full real vector space by density, so a complex embedding forces its value of c to vanish. Injectivity then 

64 forces c to vanish on that whole field factor. Thus a normalized rational positive functional exists only if some field factor is totally real. For example, Q[x]/(x3 − x − 1) has a real point but admits no such functional. This obstruction to a rational functional is compatible with the positive definite Gram matrices constructed above; the two objects solve different equations. Even the trace of a square need not be positive on an infeasible critical system. In one variable take 

F = x2 + 1, so G = x5 + (2 Q + 1) x + 1 and T = 276. Reducing h = J − T modulo G and using Newton sums gives Tr A(h2) = 5(6 Q + 3 − T )2 − 16(2 Q + 1) 3 < 0 (Q ≥ 1024) ,

although J ≥ 6Q > T on the real line. The nonreal contributions prevent this trace from being a positive sum over just the real points. These examples do not rule out approximate moment methods or algorithms using several traces. They explain why the exact SOS identity and its still-unproved succinct numerator representation cannot simply be replaced by a single positive trace assertion. 

# N.6 A degree obstruction to positive-coefficient annihilators 

Another possible route is to use the absence of negative real roots of the characteristic polynomial of h. A polynomial positive on the nonnegative real axis has a positive-coefficient Pólya multiple after multiplication by a sufficiently large power of 1 + s. The required degree, however, can be too large even for a coefficient-defined SLP. This occurs within our small-coefficient weighted critical family.

# N.7 Selective valuation promises 

The integer problem admits a more selective arithmetic promise than a short squarefree kernel. Write ⊕P for parity counting, and MP for the class defined by a selected bit of a # P function. We use the established inclusion BPP ⊕P ⊆ MP [15, Proposition 3.3(a)]; we do not identify parity counting with PP .

Proposition 8. Let an integer SLP compute an odd integer A. Suppose B and an even positive integer K

have polynomial value and are supplied with the input. Promise only that 

p ≡ 3 (mod 4) , vp(|A|) odd =⇒ p ≤ 2B and vp(|A|) < K. 

Then positivity of A is in ⊕P on this promise. If the corresponding condition holds for every odd prime, arbitrary two-adic valuation and A = 0 are allowed at the cost of a randomized many-one reduction to ⊕P,hence an MP promise algorithm. Proof. Count pairs ( p, j ) satisfying 

p ≤ 2B prime , p ≡ 3 (mod 4) , 1 ≤ j ≤ K, pj | A. 

Each pair is checked in polynomial time, since pj has at most BK +1 bits. A prime contributes min( vp(|A|), K ). Its parity is the full valuation parity: valuations at least K must be even, and K is even. Omitted primes contribute one modulo four. If a is the count, then |A| ≡ (−1) a (mod 4). Combining this bit with A mod 4 gives the sign. For the second assertion, choose a short q ≡ 7 (mod 8) coprime to A, and count instead 

aq = # {(p, j ) : 3 ≤ p ≤ 2B prime , 1 ≤ j ≤ K, p j | A, (p/q ) = −1}.

Here ( ·/q ) is the Jacobi symbol, so primality of q is unnecessary. Since (2 /q ) = 1 and ( −1/q ) = −1, (A/q ) = sgn( A)( −1) aq .

66 The coprime-modulus sieve used earlier applies to q = 8 t − 1. For completeness, if 0 < |A| < 2H , put 

h0 = ⌈log 2(H + 2) ⌉, b0 = 2 h0 + 64 , z = 2 b0 , s = 40(1 + ⌈log 2 b0⌉) + 1 , T0 = z2s+10 .

Uniform t ∈ { 1, . . . , T 0} gives Pr[gcd( A, 8t − 1) = 1] ≥ 1/(128 b30). The proof is the same odd Bonferroni truncation for small prime divisors, followed by the large-prime union bound; the progression has one forbidden residue modulo each odd prime. After 256 b30 trials, use the first coprime modulus and one parity query. Reject if there is none. Positive inputs accept with probability at least 1 − e−2; negative inputs always reject. Zero has no coprime sampled modulus and also rejects. The promise allows unrestricted even valuations, including at primes of arbitrary size. In the first assertion it also leaves every prime congruent to one modulo four unrestricted. Thus no short factorization or short squarefree kernel is needed. The existing ETR norm is odd, but oddness does not establish this valuation promise. Moreover, its residues already use a C2P oracle, so a parity decoder there would be relativized rather than an ordinary parity computation.

Floating-point evaluation of a size-s subtraction-free circuit needs only s + O(1) mantissa bits for a constant relative approximation; its binary exponent has polynomial length. satisfy |X − Y |/(X + Y ) ≥ 1/10, and X > Y exactly when A > 0. Their internal circuits may contain cancellation. By contrast, a polynomial relative gap between two genuinely subtraction-free circuits is decided in polynomial time by relative approximation. None of these observations supplies an unrestricted MP 

algorithm for PosSLP .

# O Short positive certificates and arithmetic normal forms 

The preceding section fixes the scalar denominator in a dense sum-of-squares representation. It leaves the description of the numerators unresolved. We now give two more permissive certificate formats, followed by consequences of the rational sign filter for ordinary arithmetic circuits. None of these results lowers the unrestricted containment ∃R ⊆ C4P.

# O.1 Conic identities in the critical algebra 

Call an integer polynomial circuit conic if its leaves are nonnegative integer constants and squares of arbitrary polynomial SLPs, and its remaining gates are addition and multiplication. Every value of such a circuit is nonnegative on Rm. The circuit may share subexpressions. Expanding it into a list of squares is unnecessary and can be very expensive. 

Proposition 10. Let I = ( G1, . . . , G m) be the fixed-degree pure-monic critical ideal and let h = J − T . A supplied pair of conic circuits A, B satisfying 

hB = 1 + A (mod I)

certifies that h > 0 at every real point of I. The identity has a coRP PP verifier. If polynomial-size certificates of this form exist for every NO instance, then 

∃R ⊆ coMA PP ⊆ C2P ∩ PH PP .

Proof. At a real point, A, B ≥ 0, and the identity gives hB ≥ 1. Thus both B and h are positive there. In particular, no separate unit or inverse certificate for B is needed. The residual hB − 1 − A is an ordinary short polynomial SLP. The pure-monic membership test of Theorem 13 therefore verifies its vanishing in the quotient with one-sided error. Guessing the circuits gives an MA PP procedure for NO instances, under the stated size hypothesis. Dense existence follows from the previous section. On a NO instance h − 1 is still strictly positive at all real critical points. Write c2(h − 1) = ∑ 

> j

P 2 

> j

, with the fixed dyadic scalar c. Then one can take 

B = c2, A = ( c2 − 1) + ∑

> j

P 2 

> j

.

68 The geometric-sum circuit for c2 −1 is short and monotone. The numerator description is still the missing part. Allowing a nonconstant B and a conic circuit gives more freedom than this particular dense construction. There is a direct connection to the real cone proof system of Alekseev, Grigoriev, Hirsch, and Tzameret [20]. A real-CPS refutation of Gi = 0 and −h ≥ 0 gives a conic circuit C(X, Y +, Y −, z ) that becomes −1 after substituting the equations and z = −h. Set the equation placeholders to zero; this preserves the identity modulo I. At every square gate, substitute z = −h inside the square and retain the result as a square leaf. The remaining circuit has conic coefficients in X and uses the formal variable z only through addition and multiplication. Propagate an even/odd pair through this circuit, interpreting ( U0, U 1) as U0 − hU 1. Its multiplication rule is (U0, U 1)( V0, V 1) = ( U0V0 + h2U1V1, U 0V1 + U1V0).

Both components remain conic. At the output this yields A − hB = −1 (mod I), with polynomial circuit-size overhead. This is a one-way simulation. Our verifier is allowed to check ideal membership without receiving explicit ideal multipliers. A universal polynomial-size real-CPS refutation theorem would be stronger: ordinary polynomial identity testing would already give ∃R ⊆ coMA .The conic format also permits a restricted coefficient constructor. Let A0(X, Y ) be built from Y -monomials, nonnegative constants, and squares of Y -free polynomial SLPs, using only addition and multiplication. Every 

Y -coefficient is a sum of squares in X. Thus A = [ Y α]A0, and an independently specified B of the same kind, are nonnegative by syntax. Their membership residual and its residue-pairing square use only a constant number of coefficient copies, so the same coRP PP verifier applies. Arbitrary Y -dependent square leaves would be unsound here: the coefficient of Y in ( Y − X)2 is −2X.

# O.2 Congruence of four trace forms 

There is another certificate that does not require the witness to respect multiplication in the algebra. Let 

H1 = (Tr A(eiej )) ij , Hh = (Tr A(he iej )) ij 

in the standard basis. Both are nonsingular because the algebra is reduced and h is a unit. 

Proposition 11. The element h is positive at every real point if and only if the rational quadratic forms 

H1 ⊗ I4 and Hh ⊗ I4 are isometric. Proof. If there are r real points, of which s have h < 0, the two signatures are r and r − 2s. Complex conjugate pairs contribute one positive and one negative direction to each form. An isometry therefore implies 

s = 0. Conversely, rationally diagonalize both forms. Equal signatures allow their diagonal entries ai, b i to be paired with bi/a i > 0. Write this ratio as ui/v i with positive integers ui, v i. Choose four integers whose squares sum to uivi. Quaternion multiplication by their vector divided by vi gives a rational matrix Qi

satisfying QtiQi = ( bi/a i)I4. The direct sum of these matrices, followed by the inverse diagonalizations, gives the isometry. If the form entries have bit height H, the dense isometry can be chosen with bit heights polynomial in 

D, H . To see this, first use a small integer congruence making all leading principal minors nonzero. Their product is a nonzero polynomial of degree D(D + 1) in the congruence entries, so a grid of that size suffices. Rational LDL decomposition and Hadamard’s bound control all subsequent entries. The four-square vectors above and a common denominator obey the same kind of bound. This argument establishes height bounds, not short descriptions. A supplied integer matrix B, described by a polynomial-time generator of coefficient-SLP entries, and a short nonzero scalar c, can certify 

Bt(H1 ⊗ I4)B = c2(Hh ⊗ I4).

Square and sum the integer entry residuals. Each summand contains only a constant number of matrix-entry and trace-entry coefficient copies. All indices have polynomial binary length, so one raw modular counting sum verifies the resulting integer is zero. A supplied short modulus with c̸ ≡ 0 certifies the scalar’s nonvanishing. 

69 Nonsingularity of the trace forms then forces B to be invertible. A universal short-description theorem for these witnesses would again give ∃R ⊆ coMA PP . It is not proved here. The number four has a local obstruction in the canonical family. In fact Norm A(h) ≡ 3 (mod 4). Modulo four the critical equations are p(Xi) = X5 

> i

+ Xi + 1, and the perturbation reduces to b(X) = ( X + 1)(2 X + 3). Its rank-five norm is Norm p(b) = 259 ≡ 3 (mod 4), while Tr p(b) = 15. For m ≥ 2, write a = b(X1) − T , with 

T ≡ 2 (mod 4). Taking the second-coordinate norm of a + 2 b(X2) gives a4(a + 2) (mod 4). Its first-coordinate norm is again three modulo four; all remaining norms are odd fifth powers. Odd numbers of trace-form copies therefore fail the determinant square-class condition. Numbers of copies congruent to two modulo four fail the two-adic Hasse condition (Norm A(h), −1) 2 = −1. The supplementary note gives the complete calculation and a further three-square obstruction in the algebra itself for m ≥ 3.

Proposition 12. Every number-field factor of the resulting critical algebra has a complex place. Proof. Suppose a factor K were totally real, of degree d. The coordinate images ai are algebraic integers, and every embedding of K gives a real critical point. Put p(t) = t5 + t + 1 and qi = Q0/w i. The critical equations give p(ai) = −qiFXi (a). Its integer norm is divisible by qdi , but the coordinate bound gives

The norm must vanish, so p(ai) = 0. However, 

p(t) = ( t2 + t + 1)( t3 − t2 + 1) .

The quadratic is irreducible with no real root. The cubic is irreducible by the rational-root test and has discriminant −23. Neither irreducible factor is totally real. This is impossible for an element of a totally real field. Write R for the integral critical order and A = R ⊗ Q. The element h = J − T is a unit in A and modulo two; it is not asserted to be an integral unit at every prime. 

70 Proposition 13. For every NO instance of the enlarged construction, the integral order R contains

Strong approximation away from the archimedean places therefore applies [23, Main Theorem 28.5.3]. For every finite prime impose the open condition x0g ∈ (R ⊗ Zp)4 on a norm-one element g. These sets are nonempty by the local representations, and form an adelic open set: outside finitely many primes the integral norm-one subgroup already suffices. Strong approximation gives a rational g satisfying all the conditions. The coordinates of x0g belong to R, and its norm is h. The argument uses the given order locally and does not require it to be maximal. The distinction between existence and size remains decisive. The earlier many-square construction gives explicit uniform height bounds with a fixed scalar. This four-square construction has scalar one but, as used here, no comparable uniform height bound. Neither construction gives short numerator circuits. 

# O.4 Eliminating adaptive PosSLP queries 

The real sign filter also gives a reduction normal form that is independent of our counting-hierarchy arguments. 

Lemma 25. Given positive binary integers L, B , one can construct in time polynomial in their bit lengths a rational SLP RL,B such that all denominators are positive on the real line and 

2−L ≤ | z| ≤ 2L =⇒ |RL,B (z) − sgn z| ≤ 2−B .

Proof. Use the folds ϕs(z) = 2 sz/ (1 + s2z2), with positive powers of two s. The first scale-one fold puts the absolute value in [2 −L, 1]. From [2 −K , 1], the scale s = 2 ⌈K/ 2⌉ puts it in [2 −⌈ K/ 2⌉, 1]. After O(log L) stages the interval is [1 /2, 1]. A scale-one fold now changes the error e = 1 − z to e2/(1 + z2) ≤ e2, so O(log B)further folds suffice. Every scale constant has a short repeated-squaring circuit. For z > 0, these folds have the useful stability property 

d log ϕs(z)

d log z = 1 − s2z2

1 + s2z2 ∈ [−1, 1] .

Hence any composition satisfies |R′(z)| ≤ 1/|z|. The next proof uses the stronger fact that every oracle gate can reset its error using the filter’s uniform guarantee. 

Theorem 17. Every language in PPosSLP is polynomial-time many-one reducible to PosSLP .

71 Proof. Unroll a polynomial-time oracle computation as a polynomial-size Boolean circuit with oracle gates. Pad every query to one fixed polynomial length q, and let G bound the number of ordinary Boolean gates. There is a polynomial-size polynomial circuit Wq (b) that evaluates an encoded SLP on Boolean descriptions: it returns 2 Zb − 1 on a valid description of value Zb, and −1 on an invalid description. Decode each instruction by Boolean polynomials and use polynomial-size multiplexers over the preceding registers. On every Boolean input the output is a nonzero odd integer with the desired oracle sign. Before choosing any approximation precision, bound this fixed evaluator and its derivatives. The usual arithmetic-circuit recurrences give a computable L = 2 poly( n) such that on [ −1, 2] q ,

|Wq (b)| ≤ 2L, ∑

> i

|∂iWq (b)| ≤ 2L.

The integer L has polynomial binary length. Put B = L + 2 G + 20 and η = 2 −B . Replace Boolean NOT and AND by 1 − a and ab , and replace each oracle gate by 1 + RL+2 ,B +2 (Wq (b)) 2 .

Inductively, every wire stays within ρ = 4 Gη < 1/4 of its ideal Boolean value. An ordinary gate increases the current error by at most four. At an oracle gate the evaluator error is at most 2 Lρ = 2 −20 . Its ideal value is odd, so the perturbed value has the same sign and lies in the filter’s permitted range. The filter resets the output error to at most η. This induction allows arbitrary adaptive dependencies. The final rational value exceeds 1 /2 precisely on accepting inputs. Positive-denominator clearing produces an integer SLP for this comparison. Its size is polynomial because every filter parameter has polynomial binary length. The order of the bounds in this proof matters. Bounding derivatives of the entire precision-dependent simulation would create a circular accuracy requirement. Only the fixed universal evaluator is bounded before precision is selected. The theorem gives one query in place of adaptive queries; it does not remove an oracle level from the existing upper bound for PosSLP . In particular, ordinary SLP values cannot be substituted for arbitrary GapP values.
