Proposition 14. The sign problem for polynomial-size circuits with rational leaves, arithmetic operations, exact division, and nested principal square roots is polynomial-time many-one equivalent to PosSLP . One may either promise valid divisions and radicands, or reject invalid circuits as part of the language. Proof. Let s be the number of gates and n the input length. Every defined gate has algebraic degree at most 2 s and absolute logarithmic height at most ( n + 1)2 s. The standard height inequalities for addition, multiplication, division, and square roots give these bounds directly. Hence 

H = ( n + 2)4 s+1 

bounds the logarithmic magnitude of every nonzero gate in both directions: 2 −H ≤ | v| ≤ 2H .Put c0 = 3 H + 4, and prescribe the error schedule 

Kj = 2 s−j (H + 2 c0 + 10) − 2c0, Ej = 2 −Kj , 0 ≤ j ≤ s. 

72 Then Kj = Kj−1/2 − c0 and Ks = H + 10. Approximate each gate by a rational SLP. Addition and multiplication amplify the previous error by at most 2 H+3 , and a valid division by at most 2 3H+2 . For a radicand a ≥ 0 with approximation ˜a, use a′ = ˜a + 2 Ej−1 > 0. The square-root circuit above can approximate √a′ within Ej−1/4, while 

|√a′ − √a| ≤ √3Ej−1.

Every new error is therefore at most 2 c0

√Ej−1 = Ej . This includes zero radicands. All exponents have polynomial binary length, so the complete rational approximation has polynomial circuit size. The output error is at most 2 −H−10 . Its comparison with 2 −H−1 has the sign of the true positivity predicate and gives one PosSLP instance after denominator clearing. To reject invalid circuits, test the approximants of each divisor and radicand against the same separated thresholds before using them. These are polynomially many PosSLP queries, which Theorem 17 combines into one. The reverse reduction uses no radical gates. This statement concerns exact rational division. Integer division and floor are different operations and are not included. 

# O.6 Polynomial-degree real-root gates 

The square-root result extends to more general specified real algebraic roots. We give the arithmetic-operation argument because a bit-complexity bound polynomial in coefficient height would be insufficient: ordinary SLP coefficients can have exponentially many bits. 

Theorem 18. Consider a polynomial-size arithmetic DAG with rational constants, arithmetic operations, exact division, and real-root gates. Each root gate lists polynomially many earlier values a0, . . . , a d and an index j, and returns the j-th distinct real root of ∑dk=0 akXk, in increasing order. The output-sign problem is in PPosSLP , and hence is polynomial-time many-one reducible to PosSLP . Invalid operations or root specifications can be rejected. 

Floor-free rational isolation. First let the coefficients be ordinary rational SLP values. Polynomial-degree Euclidean and Sturm computations use polynomially many field operations and PosSLP tests. Compute the square-free part before isolating its roots. Let its degree be d, let all roots have magnitude at most 2 Γ,and let their separation be at least σ.We adapt the accelerated subdivision analysis of Sagraloff and Mehlhorn [22]. An active interval I = ( a, b )has width w, nonroot endpoints, and a parameter NI = 2 2ℓI , with ℓI ≥ 1. Exact Sturm counts discard empty intervals and return single-root intervals. A successful accelerated step retains all real roots in an interval of width in [ w/ (8 NI ), w/N I ], and increments ℓI . Otherwise split in the middle half and decrement ℓI , with minimum one. The published Newton test uses a floor to locate a small interval on a very fine grid. We avoid that integer. Try each multiplicity k = 1 , . . . , d and each quarter point ξ with P ′(ξ)̸ = 0, and form 

λ = ξ − kP (ξ)/P ′(ξ), r = w/ (4 NI ).

Clip [ λ−r, λ +r] to the parent, rejecting width below 3 r/ 4. Snap any endpoint within r/ 2 of the corresponding parent boundary to that boundary. Move each remaining endpoint by at most r/ 16 to avoid roots; a grid of 

d + 1 points suffices. Accept if the exact real-root count is unchanged. The final width lies in [5 r/ 8, 25 r/ 8], and every endpoint that moves does so by at least 7 r/ 16, which is at least one eighth of the final width. Also test intervals of width between 11 w/ (16 NI ) and 13 w/ (16 NI ) adjacent to either parent boundary, using nonroot endpoints. These modifications preserve the geometric invariants of [22, Lemmas 24–26]. In particular, if a cluster lies in a subinterval of width at most w/ (8192 NI ) and all other complex roots are at distance at least 1024 dN I w,one trial with its true multiplicity puts λ within w/ (128 NI ) of the cluster center. The clipped interval retains it, so the exact count accepts. The endpoint-movement bound is the other essential invariant in the 

73 nonsplitting-chain argument. Count splitting nodes by losses or divisions of the complex-root sets in the one-circle disks, not just by changes in real-root counts. There are O(d) such nodes. The resulting forest has 

O(d [log( d + 1) + log(Γ + log(1 /σ ) + 2)]) nodes. A single strict Cauchy interval suffices for initialization. All endpoints are short rational SLPs; no long floor is written. The supplementary root-isolation note details the invariant transfer and provides an independent geometric audit. To refine to error 2 −B , choose ε < min(2 −B−2, σ/ 4) and isolate the roots of 

P (X − ε)P (X)P (X + ε).

Every original real root lies between its two shifted copies. Its isolating interval therefore has width below 2 ε.The threefold degree increase and the short power-of-two shift preserve the arithmetic bound, polynomial in 

d and the binary lengths of the magnitude, separation, and precision exponents. 

Nested coefficients and stable root choices. We explain why nesting does not cause repeated circuit-size or precision blowup. Let N bound the input DAG size and all listed degrees. Its selected roots generate a field of degree at most N N . Standard denominator and conjugate bounds give a computable 

H0 = 2 poly( N ) which bounds all semantic gate magnitudes, inverse nonzero magnitudes, root separations, and the conditioning of polynomial-degree gcd computations. These bounds include the auxiliary rational expressions in every possible Euclidean branch, whose sizes have one polynomial bound. For example, integer norms clear inverse denominators and bound nonzero values away from zero; a root gate increases field degree by at most its listed degree. Two roots require an extension of degree at most d2 over the coefficient field to bound their difference. No full splitting field is needed. Use approximations to the earlier selected roots to determine the true degree and square-free Euclidean branch, comparing tested quantities with ±2−H0−1. Values determined to be zero are set to literal zero in that branch. Then evaluate the selected square-free coefficient formulas at the earlier rational approximants. This produces a rational perturbation Q of the true square-free polynomial P ; it does not square-free an arbitrary perturbation of a multiple-root polynomial. Suppose the leading coefficient of P has magnitude at least 2 −H0 , its roots have magnitude at most 2 H0 ,and their separation is at least 2 −H0 . On the circle of radius η = 2 −B−2 < 2−H0−2 about a root, 

|P (z)| ≥ 2−H0 η(2 −H0−1)d−1.

A coefficient error at most 2 −B−8( d+1)( H0+1) is smaller than this margin after evaluation. Rouché’s theorem gives exactly one Q-root in each circle. Conjugation shows that real roots stay real; nonreal roots have imaginary part at least half the separation and stay nonreal. Thus real-root counts and their ordering are preserved. If there are r root gates, put 

C = 100( N + 1)( H0 + 1) , Bi = ( r − i + 1) C. 

Cache one approximation to gate i, with error below 2 −Bi . Earlier errors are smaller by a factor 2 −C , which pays for coefficient conditioning and the Rouché margin. Rational isolation of Qi then produces the required cache. Apply its geometric bound using the magnitude and separation just proved, rather than the artificial expanded denominators of the cached approximants. Each root gate consequently adds only polynomially many arithmetic operations. Earlier DAG nodes are shared, not copied or recursively recomputed. The final gap and conditioning bounds determine the output sign by PosSLP queries. Theorem 17 combines them into one. Polynomial-value degree is essential to this proof: Sturm sequences, the multiplicity trials, and the subdivision forest all pay for the degree. The exponentially large characteristic polynomial in the general ETR construction is outside its scope. There is also an exact-witness obstruction. The bounded quadratic system 1 ≤ xi ≤ 2, xi+1 = x2 

> i

(0 ≤ i < m ), x2 

> m

= x0 + 1 

74 has a unique solution in this box. Its first coordinate has minimal polynomial XD − X − 1, D = 2 m+1 ,whose Galois group is SD by Selmer and Osada [26, 27]. In a tower of root adjunctions of degree at most d,the normal-closure Galois group has no prime divisor greater than d: each step embeds its new kernel in a product of subgroups of Sd. A field containing this first coordinate has normal closure containing its splitting field. Bertrand’s postulate therefore forces d > D/ 2. Thus low-degree root gates do not describe every exact bounded ETR witness. This obstruction does not apply to approximation. For the same example put u = D(x0 − 1). Its root lies in (3 /5, 1) for D ≥ 4, and Newton iteration from one on (1 + u/D )D − 2 − u/D 

has error et+1 ≤ 2e2 

> t

, since its derivative is at least 3 /4 and its second derivative is below three there. Each iteration has a short powering circuit. It yields rational SLP approximations to all the coordinates to error 2−H in O(log H) iterations, even though the exact-root description requires a large-degree gate. The example is also trivially feasible; no decision lower bound is implied. 

# O.7 A stochastic-system application 

Etessami, Stewart, and Yannakakis already prove that the strict rational threshold problem for the least fixed point of a probabilistic polynomial system is many-one equivalent to PosSLP [24, Appendix C]. Their exact Newton iteration has a polynomial warmup followed by quadratic convergence, and hence reaches error 2−H in poly( n, log H) arithmetic operations. This is a published direct route to PosSLP, independent of our norm construction. We use their proof also for signs of coordinate differences. After removing coordinates equal to zero or one, the least fixed point is nonsingular. Adjoining y = xi − xj preserves isolation, so the same algebraic separation and Newton bounds give exact difference signs in PPosSLP .

Proposition 15. Rational least-fixed-point thresholds for maxPPSs, minPPSs, and two-player branching simple stochastic games belong to 

NP PosSLP ∩ coNP PosSLP .

Using our PosSLP bounds, they consequently belong to both MA PP ∩ coMA PP and ΣPP  

> 2

∩ ΠPP  

> 2

.Proof. For a one-player system, use the polynomial-time qualitative algorithm to remove zero and one coordinates, leaving 0 < q ∗ < 1. Guess a pure policy σ, let z be the PPS least fixed point after fixing it, and verify 0 < z < 1 and P (z) = z. In simple normal form, a choice row is max( xi, x j ) or min( xi, x j ), so policy consistency uses only the coordinate comparisons just described. For maxPPS, z ≤ q∗ by the policy comparison, and q∗ ≤ z by leastness. For minPPS, take an optimal policy τ , for which qτ = q∗. Then Pτ (z) ≥ z, and the exact quadratic identity gives (I − P ′ 

> τ

(( q∗ + z)/2))( z − q∗) = z − Pτ (z) ≤ 0.

The inverse in [25, Theorem 4.6(i)] is nonnegative for every 0 ≤ z < 1. Thus z ≤ q∗, and equality again follows. An optimal policy supplies a valid certificate for either threshold side. For two players, optimal pure memoryless policies exist [25, Section 5]. To certify a lower threshold, guess max’s policy and a one-player certificate for the resulting minPPS. For the complementary upper threshold, guess min’s policy and a certificate for the resulting maxPPS. These existential witnesses concatenate. They do not require an identity such as NP NP A

= NP A.Finally, a fixed complete witness can be checked with bounded error using our BPP PP PosSLP algorithm, giving the two Merlin–Arthur inclusions. Substituting PosSLP ⊆ PNP PP 

gives the displayed second-level polynomial-hierarchy inclusions. The least-fixed-point condition in this certificate is essential. For example, x = max( x, 1/2) has every point of [1 /2, 1] as a fixed point. Its least fixed point is only 1 /2. These stochastic systems provide an application below the general ETR bound; no reduction of arbitrary ETR to them is established. 

75 O.8 Boolean oracle computations above ETR 

The randomized ETR bound also has consequences for stronger Boolean oracle classes, without improving its numerical level. 

Corollary 3. The proposed ETR containment implies 

BPP ∃R ⊆ BPP C3P, PP ∃R ⊆ C4P, PH ∃R ⊆ PC4P ⊆ C5P.

Proof. Amplify every ETR oracle simulation and take a union bound over the polynomially many adaptive queries. This proves the first inclusion. For the second, a PP machine with p(n) random bits has acceptance probabilities on the grid 2 −p(n). Shift its decision threshold from 1 /2 to 1 /2 + 2 −p(n)−1, which preserves its language and gives a margin 2 −p(n)−1 on both sides, including ties. Amplify the ETR simulations until their total error is below 2 −p(n)−3. The resulting PP C3P machine has the same threshold decision. This is the usual relativizing lowness of BPP for PP, applied with the existing counting oracle. Finally, relativized Toda gives PH ∃R ⊆ PPP ∃R

, and the second inclusion finishes the proof. These are Boolean oracle computations. They do not establish a containment for alternating blocks of real quantifiers, and they do not assert lowness for every level of a fully relativized counting hierarchy. In particular, the concrete equality supplied by the proof is PP C3P⊕∃ R = C4P.

# O.10 Fixed blocks of real quantifiers 

There is a separate extension on the real side. A fixed number of quantifier blocks is different from a Boolean oracle computation over ∃R: the parameters of a later block may themselves be algebraic sample points. Two additional interfaces make this distinction manageable. We describe them here; the supplementary block-elimination notes give the full construction and its specialization audit. 

Theorem 19. For each fixed w, truth of integer polynomial sentences with w blocks of real quantifiers belongs to a fixed level of CH. Consequently the discrete, constant-free real polynomial hierarchy belongs to CH. 

Here the input sentence has polynomial description length; the number of variables in a block is unrestricted. The terminology for the discrete real polynomial hierarchy is that of [29]. The following argument establishes the structural containment; Section 4 gives its explicit numerical refinement. 

Short addresses for real roots. Let q be square-free of degree at most D, with positive leading coefficient. Its coefficients may be integers of singly exponential bit length, or bounded rational expressions in a fixed number of previously selected algebraic roots. In the latter case assume a CH oracle for signs of the resulting bounded polynomial expressions in those earlier roots. This is stronger than knowing the signs of the individual input coefficients. All degree and expression-height bounds are singly exponential, and the indexed integer coefficient arrays belong uniformly to one fixed level of CH. Use the full derivative Thom vector of a real root α and the integer hashes 

Tp,a (α) = 

> D

∑

> j=1

(aj mod p) sgn( q(j)(α)) , D3 < p < 2( D3 + 1) , 0 ≤ a < p. 

Enumerating all integer moduli in this interval is harmless. At least one is prime, and the usual polynomial hash argument gives an injective member: each pair of Thom vectors excludes at most D parameters modulo that prime. For the constant-depth implementation, use the product filter 

P (y) = 

> L

∏

> k=−L

(2 max(0 ,−k)y + 2 max(0 ,k ))m, R(y) = P (y) − P (−y)

P (y) + P (−y) .

Here 2 −L ≤ | v| ≤ 2L bounds every nonzero derivative or test value, Pmax = 2( D3 + 1), and m =

⌈log 2(64 DP max + 64) ⌉. The denominator is positive on the real line, R(0) = 0, and the error from the sign of each nonzero value is below 1 /(16 DP max ). One dyadic scale within a factor of two supplies a factor at most 1/3 in |P (−y)/P (y)|. The degree, height, and number of factors are singly exponential, while the product has constant unbounded-fan-in arithmetic depth. Thus membership in a hash bucket becomes a strict polynomial sign at the roots of q. If h is negative exactly on that bucket and positive at the other real roots, the sign of the trailing nonzero coefficient of Res X (q(X), h (X) + u)is ( −1) to its cardinality. Zeros at nonreal roots occur in conjugate pairs and do not change this sign. If nt

are the bucket sizes and C is the number of odd buckets, then

where r is the number of real roots. Consequently the maximum of C over the hash family is r, and every 

maximizing hash is injective. Choose the first such hash. An occupied bucket is now a short, coherent name for one real root. 

77 To test g(α) < 0, keep this same hash and bucket and add a rational sign filter for g to the bucket predicate. The corresponding parity is the desired truth value. An exponentially indexed family of test polynomials therefore has a coherent CH-accessible sign profile; one never guesses the whole profile. Resultants, polynomial products, and square-free preprocessing have a constant-depth implementation. For the last operation, the specialized trailing- and leading-coefficient selections in [10, Lemma 6.13] are made using the earlier sample’s sign oracle. A fixed number of algebraic extensions adds only a fixed number of CH levels. The domain conditions matter. A zero specialized defining polynomial is discarded; a nonzero constant gives no root addresses. For rational coordinates gi(α)/g 0(α), test g0(α)̸ = 0 at the addressed root; global coprimality of q and g0 is unnecessary and can discard valid samples. Clear denominators by even powers on this domain. 

Uniform block sampling. The remaining task is to supply the sample polynomials without a sequential elimination computation of exponential length. The block elimination construction of Basu, Pollack, and Roy [28, Algorithms 14.1 and 14.2] provides suitable geometry. Its algebraic operations require the following replacements. First, let Q be a nonnegative sampling polynomial with zero set in the ball of radius 1 /c , where 0 < c ≤ 1. Choose an even d > deg X Q + 2 and set

Its homogenizations have no common point at infinity. The AGS resultant therefore gives its norm polynomial and, by derivatives with respect to a linear projection, its coordinate numerators. The scalar denominator depends only on the deformation parameters, not on the free real parameters. Enumerate the projection choices, infinitesimal coefficient indices, and derivative orders used for limits. For each fixed parameter value, a separating choice supplies the required bounded limits. Repeated limiting roots are handled by the appropriate derivatives and the rootwise denominator guard. Other choices can supply extra ambient real points; these cannot create a sign condition that is not realized in the fiber. Second, restricted subresultant elimination can be replaced by coefficient lists of ordinary resultants. For nonzero f, g over a characteristic-zero field and an independent indeterminate λ,deg gcd( f, g ) = ord t Res X (f (X) + t, g (X) + λt ).

At a common root of multiplicities m, n , the local order is min( m, n ); the indeterminate λ prevents cancellation when the two orders coincide. Include every degree truncation to handle specialization drops. Keeping the degrees and these gcd degrees constant preserves the real root branches and all required signs on each connected parameter cell. All of these coefficient lists use a fixed number of resultant and coefficient-selection layers. Each admissible root branch and its rational image are continuous on such a cell. Their fixed input signs therefore keep the image in one connected sign component, on which the continuation truth is constant. This supplies the inductive invariant for subsequent blocks; equality of disconnected fibers’ sign sets alone would not suffice. Finally, fix the number w of real quantifier blocks. All the degree, height, and family-size bounds after w block eliminations are singly exponential in the input length. A full sample tuple has w root variables. Represent a node by the appropriate coordinate prefix of a full valid tuple, and match parent and child by coordinate equality. Expanding the finite choices of representation indices in the resulting truth formula still gives singly exponential size. The formula uses at most w2 root variables and 2 w infinitesimals. Effective complete CAD in this fixed dimension bounds all projection degrees and coefficient heights by singly exponential quantities D∗, H ∗. We use this bound without computing the CAD. 

78 Put r = 2 w, M = rD ∗ + 2, and B∗ = H∗ + ⌈r log 2(D∗ + 1) ⌉ + 5. Set η = 2 −B∗ and

These constants have short rational SLPs. On the connected region 0 < ϵ 1 < 2η and 0 < ϵ i+1 < ϵ Mi , each nonleading infinitesimal monomial is at most ϵ1 times the leading one. The degree and height bounds make the leading term dominate in every nonzero cumulative projection polynomial in the infinitesimal variables. The region consequently lies in one open infinitesimal-base CAD cell. The formal infinitesimal tuple and the displayed finite tuple give the same sampled-tree truth there, using real-closed-field transfer for the formal tuple. This step precedes hashing and preserves truth and validity; it does not assert a finite real gap for genuinely infinitesimal values. At the finite specialization, apply the root-address construction successively along each full sample tuple. Each tuple has polynomial description length, and all its atom signs and rootwise validity conditions are available in CH. Coordinate comparison between two tuples uses at most 2 w successive root extensions and remains in CH. Replace each real block by the corresponding Boolean choice of a full valid tuple with the required coordinate prefix. Invalid choices are excluded by conjunction for an existential block and implication for a universal block. Duplicate representations do not affect either quantifier. The fixed number of Boolean quantifiers still gives a language in CH, proving Theorem 19. This construction is intended for each fixed number of real blocks. It gives no bound independent of w,and no improvement to the numerical C4P bound for the existential case. The supplementary notes separate the root-address lemma, the block sampling construction, and the finite-specialization argument so that these additional interfaces can be reviewed independently. 

# P Structural affine depth and restricted integer algorithms 

The next refinements concern the dependence on the number of real blocks and several restricted arithmetic constructions. They do not lower the general bounds ∃R ⊆ C4P and PosSLP ⊆ BPP PP ∩ PNP PP 

.

# P.1 An affine level for real quantifier blocks 

Theorem 20. There are absolute constants A, B such that, for every fixed w, truth of integer polynomial sentences with w real quantifier blocks belongs to CAw +B P.

This structural proof first gives unspecified absolute constants. The evaluator refinement and numerical ledger in Section 4 give the explicit choice A = 9 , B = 17. The degree of the input-size polynomial can still depend on w.

Proof. We strengthen the representation invariant used for root addresses. Keep every expression as a division-free integer polynomial circuit in the earlier root variables. 80 P.2 Recovering sparse signed digits 

The next promise removes the unknown scale by recovering all occupied digit positions. A nonadjacent signed binary expansion, or NAF, has digits in {− 1, 0, 1}, with no two adjacent nonzero digits. Every integer has a unique such finite expansion. Its weight is the number of nonzero digits; the positions are encoded in binary. 

Theorem 21. Given an integer SLP and a polynomial-valued bound t, its complete NAF can be recovered in deterministic polynomial time under the promise that its weight is at most t. The digit positions may be exponentially large and need not be supplied. In particular, promised positivity belongs to P.Proof. Fix a uniform upper bound H ≥ 1 on the NAF positions, with log H polynomial in the input length. For odd ℓ ≥ 3, consider signed digit vectors on a cycle of length ℓ, again with no adjacent nonzeros, now including the first and last positions. They represent the residues modulo 2 ℓ − 1 bijectively. For injectivity, any two supports have a common zero, since each has at most ( ℓ − 1) /2 positions. Rotate that zero to the highest position. Each represented integer then has absolute value less than 2 ℓ/3, so their difference has absolute value less than 2 ℓ − 1. Congruence forces equality as integers, and ordinary NAF uniqueness forces equality of the vectors. The number of cyclic vectors is 2 ℓ + ( −1) ℓ = 2 ℓ − 1: it is the trace of the ℓ-th power of the transfer matrix ( 1 2 1 0 ). Thus injectivity gives bijectivity. To decode a canonical residue 

r, compute the ordinary NAFs of r and r − (2 ℓ − 1). Exactly one has length at most ℓ and satisfies cyclic nonadjacency. This takes polynomial time in ℓ.Write the unknown expansion as Z = ∑si=1 ϵi2ei , with s ≤ t. Call an odd prime p good if no difference 

ei − ej is 0 , 1, or −1 modulo p. There are at most 

B = 3 

(t

2

)

⌈log 2(H + 1) ⌉

bad primes. Indeed every bad prime divides one of the nonzero integers ei − ej or ei − ej ± 1; nonadjacency makes these integers nonzero. Choose 2 B + 1 small odd base primes. More than half are good. For a good p, the cyclic decoder modulo 2p − 1 returns the original signs and the distinct positions ei mod p. Choose distinct small odd primes q̸ = p

with product greater than H. The same no-collision and no-adjacency conditions hold modulo pq . Decode modulo 2 pq − 1, match each term to its position modulo p, and obtain ei mod q. CRT recovers every ei

exactly. For bad p, either discard an invalid reconstruction or keep its proposed expansion after checking the weight, position bounds, signs, and nonadjacency. Every good base prime returns the same correct sorted list. Its strict majority therefore determines the output, without an equality oracle to certify the bad-prime lists. All primes and the products pq have polynomial numerical value. The queried Mersenne moduli consequently have polynomial bit length, and an SLP is evaluated modulo each in polynomial time. The most significant signed digit determines the sign. The total language asserting that an SLP output is positive and has NAF weight at most t is in coRP .Run the decoder with fixed polynomial bounds, check that its proposed expansion has the required form and sign, and compare the resulting short SLP with the input using the usual one-sided modular equality test. Every true instance gives the correct candidate. On a false instance a candidate of the prescribed form cannot equal the output. The same proof recognizes the sparsity condition without the sign restriction. For specified SLP coefficients, the modular values have raw GapP representatives instead of polynomial-time residues. The reconstruction uses a fixed polynomial list of moduli. Pack all positive and negative branch counts for these representatives into one # P value, using polynomially wide fields. One exact retrieval gives the entire list, after which reconstruction is ordinary polynomial-time computation. Thus the sparse-coefficient promise is in P#P[1] ⊆ PPP . One retrieval of a count is not being identified with one PP threshold question. 

An unknown sparse multiple. The same method works when a supplied polynomial-size list of positive sparse multipliers contains some Di for which DiZ has weight at most t. Decode every product to get a list of candidate fractions Ni/D i, one of which is right. Every pairwise cross-difference NiDj − Nj Di is a sum of polynomially many signed powers of two; normalization to NAF does not increase their number. The small Mersenne moduli from the preceding proof give a deterministic hitting set for every nonzero such 

81 difference. Test ZD i ≡ Ni at this entire set. A true candidate survives, and two unequal candidate fractions cannot both survive. This determines the value and its sign. In particular, the multiplier list can be Dp = 2 p − 1 for 1 ≤ p ≤ P , with P polynomially bounded. This handles an unknown short period even when the output itself has exponentially many nonzero signed digits. The promise remains essential; no such sparsity theorem is asserted for general SLP outputs or for the selector norms in the ETR proof.

# P.4 Approximate squares with exact integral identities 

The integral four-square theorem still leaves the size of its numerator circuits open. There is a short approximation construction that controls both real and dyadic errors. Its exact identities also clarify the remaining obstacle. Retain the bounded critical objective and put h = Jw − T . Define 

a0 = X31 + X1 + 1 , a = a0 + 2 M, ∆ = 4 h − 3a2.

The identity 

a20 − (X61 + 3( X1 + 1) 2) = 2 (X41 + X31 − (X1 + 1) 2)

shows that K0 = ( a2 − h)/2 is an ordinary integer polynomial with a short circuit. Thus ∆ = a2 − 8K0.Modulo the critical ideal and two, a2 = h = X1 + 1, a unit. Choose M = B3 + B + 1, where B = 2 s + 3 is the real critical-box bound. Then 1 ≤ a ≤ 3M there. Increasing Q by a known power of two of polynomial numerical value makes h ≥ 9M 2 on infeasible instances, so h ≤ ∆ ≤ 4h there. The earlier separation and real-box arguments remain valid.

84 P.5 A limit of a single modular counting gap 

There is an exact obstruction to one direct flattening proposal. The model hides an integer Z and supplies only its canonical residues modulo polynomial-bit integers. A counting path makes polynomially many such queries, possibly adaptively, and contributes +1 or −1. Fix a nondeterministic string and its full residue-answer transcript. The compatible integers form either the empty set or one residue class, by generalized CRT. Thus the gap has the form 

g(Z) = ∑ 

> τ∈T

ϵτ 1Z≡aτ (mod dτ ), |T | ≤ 2poly( n).

Any one residue class has counts differing by at most one in the two intervals [1 , M ] and [ −M, −1]. Conse-quently ∣∣∣∣∣

> M

∑

> z=1

g(z) −

> −1

∑

> z=−M

g(z)

∣∣∣∣∣ ≤ |T | .

If g(z) > 0 exactly when z > 0, integrality makes the left side at least M . One such gap therefore cannot decide all signs over the doubly exponential range of possible hidden integers: that would require M ≤ 2poly( n).This statement concerns the specified residue-only model. It does not give a lower bound against an algorithm inspecting the SLP, against multiple PP queries, or against nonlinear processing of a retrieved count. It explains why simply summing all modular branches and taking the resulting gap’s sign cannot provide a universal sign decoder. The randomized phase algorithm and the restricted reconstruction theorems use operations outside that proposal. 

# Q Further root operations and certificate targets 

The unrestricted containments remain those of Theorems 5 and 2. We can nevertheless enlarge the class of algebraic computations handled by the integer argument. The degrees of the new root operations have binary encodings, and may be exponentially large. A separate certificate refinement removes an unnecessary constant from the exact norm target. 

# Q.1 Binary-degree radicals 

Consider arithmetic circuits with rational constants and operations + , −, ×, ÷, together with N -th root gates whose positive integer degrees N are written in binary. An even-degree gate takes the nonnegative real root; an odd-degree gate takes the unique real root. Negative even radicands and zero divisors are invalid. 

Theorem 23. Sign and validity for these circuits belong to PPosSLP . By the adaptive-query compilation theorem, they reduce many-one to PosSLP , and hence belong to BPP PP ∩ PNP PP 

.

The point is to avoid a Newton transient proportional to N . Suppose first that a > 0 is rational, given by an SLP, and 2 −L ≤ a ≤ 2L. Binary search with PosSLP comparisons finds the integer k such that 2N k ≤ a < 2N (k+1) .

This takes O(log L) comparisons. The powers have short rational SLPs because N k has O(log N + log L) bits. Writing r = a1/N , bisect [2 k, 2k+1 ] another ⌈log 2 N ⌉ + 4 times. Its upper endpoint u then has relative error 0 ≤ ε = u/r − 1 ≤ 116 N .

Every comparison uses O(log N ) multiplication gates. For the exact Newton step 

u′ = (N − 1) u + a/u N −1

N ,

weighted AM–GM and Taylor’s theorem give 0 ≤ u′

r − 1 = (N − 1)(1 + ε) + (1 + ε)−(N −1) 

N − 1 ≤ N − 12 ε2. (64) 

85 Indeed the second derivative of (1 + t)−(N −1) is at most N (N − 1) for t ≥ 0. Thus η = N ε/ 2 satisfies η′ ≤ η2,starting below 1 /32. After t steps, 0 ≤ u − r ≤ 2L2−5·2t

.

Only O(log( L + H + 1)) steps are needed for error below 2 −H . Neither N nor H needs to have polynomial value. The cases N = 1, a = 0, and negative odd radicands are immediate. For nesting, fix bounds from the original circuit before choosing any approximation precision. If s is its number of gates and b = ∑

> i

⌈log 2 Ni⌉, every gate belongs to a field of degree at most 2 b. Absolute logarithmic height satisfies 

h(x + y) ≤ h(x) + h(y) + 1 , h(xy ), h (x/y ) ≤ h(x) + h(y), h( N

√x) = h(x)/N. 

Consequently a computable L0 = 2 poly( n), with polynomial binary length, bounds every nonzero defined gate by 2 −L0 ≤ | z| ≤ 2L0 . Let M = 2 L0 . Once cached approximations have error below 1 /(4 M ), comparison with 

±1/(2 M ) identifies exact zero and strict sign. Zero radicands are therefore returned exactly. On a nonzero radicand’s neighborhood, the derivative of its chosen real root has magnitude at most 2 M , independently of 

N .Ordinary arithmetic amplifies an error e by at most 4 M 3. Using local root error 2 −B , with 

B = s(4 L0 + 5) + L0 + 20 ,

keeps the total error below 1 /(8 M ) at every gate. The preceding domain tests and the final positivity comparison are therefore valid. All approximation SLPs share the cached earlier gates. Their sizes and the number of oracle calls are polynomial, proving the first assertion. The many-one conclusion uses the earlier compilation theorem separately. 

# Q.2 Inverting a polynomial given by a monotone circuit 

The same estimate applies to a larger family. An inverse gate supplies a univariate polynomial F by a circuit using addition and multiplication, X, and nonnegative coefficient leaves. The coefficient leaves and a target b

may be values of earlier gates. The gate returns the unique r ≥ 0 satisfying F (r) = b. It is valid when F is nonconstant and b ≥ F (0). The entire internal circuit is counted in the input length; its expanded coefficient list is not part of the input. 

Theorem 24. Sign and validity for arithmetic circuits with polynomially many nested inverse gates of this form reduce many-one to PosSLP .

For F (X) = ∑Dj=0 aj Xj , aj ≥ 0, one has 

F ′(x) > 0, F ′′ (x) ≥ 0, xF ′′ (x) ≤ (D − 1) F ′(x) (x > 0) .

If F (r) = b, r > 0, and u ≥ r, then

Thus the relative error obeys the same bound as (64), with D in place of N . After an exponent search and 

O(log D) bisections, Newton’s method converges quadratically. Automatic differentiation supplies F ′ with a polynomial-size circuit, even when F has exponentially many terms. Nonconstancy is tested by F ′(1) > 0. 

86 We give the stability argument needed for nested gates. It is not enough to bound the expanded heights of the eventual rational approximants. An internal circuit of size S has degree at most 2 S . If its coefficient leaves have height at most T , ordinary coefficientwise sum and product bounds give max

Augment the semantic description by the arithmetic computations of F (0), F ′(1), b − F (0), and F ′(r). Its size is still polynomial. Fix L = 2 poly( n) such that every nonzero augmented gate lies in magnitude between 2−L and 2 L. In particular this bounds F ′(r) below at each positive inverse root. This derivative value is used to prove a bound; it is not an oracle required before computing the root. Put M = 2 L. The circuit magnitude and derivative recurrence supplies a computable G = 2 poly( n) such that ∑

when |x| ≤ 2M and each coefficient leaf cj has magnitude at most 2 M . These are derivatives with respect to the supplied leaves, so no coefficient expansion is needed. Exact zero leaves are identified from the semantic gap and replaced by zero; positive leaves retain their signs. The perturbed addition-and-multiplication circuit then has the same coefficient support as the true polynomial. Suppose its leaves and target have error at most e. For 0 < t ≤ r/ (4 D), the nonnegative derivative coefficients and Bernoulli’s inequality imply 

F ′(x) ≥ 12 F ′(r) (r − t ≤ x ≤ r).

preserves both endpoint signs. The perturbed inverse root therefore exists, lies in [1 /(2 M ), 2M ], and differs from r by at most 4 e2G+L. A true zero inverse is recognized through b − F (0) = 0 and returned exactly. Choose a common error amplification bound K = 2 G+3 L+10 , and local root precision 2 −B , where 

B = s(G + 3 L + 11) + G + 2 L + n + 30 .

The accumulated error after j ≤ s gates is at most (1 + K + · · · + Kj−1)2 −B . It is below both the displayed stability requirement and 1 /(8 M ). This justifies the domain tests, all perturbed inverse calls, and the final sign test by induction. The root algorithms depend polynomially on the binary length of B. Sharing earlier approximations gives a polynomial-size rational DAG, computed in PPosSLP . Compilation proves the theorem. These gates do not solve an arbitrary coupled system of monotone equations. They invert one variable at a time in a supplied circuit. No reduction of unrestricted ETR to this representation is known here. 

# Q.3 Sparse real-root gates of binary degree 

The root-operation model can be enlarged beyond polynomial-degree polynomials. A sparse real-root gate 

specifies 

p(X) = 

> k

∑

> j=1

aj Xej

87 and the index of a distinct real root, in increasing order. Exponents are nonnegative integers written in binary, and coefficients are values of earlier arithmetic or root gates. Thus the degree can be exponential in the circuit description, while the displayed number of terms is polynomial. A zero polynomial, a nonexistent root index, or an ordinary division by zero makes the circuit invalid. 

Theorem 25. Validity and output sign for polynomial-size circuits with nested sparse real-root gates are in 

PPosSLP . Consequently, using the adaptive PosSLP compilation established above, these problems reduce in polynomial time to PosSLP .

Multiple roots are permitted. The theorem does not require an algorithm that expands a polynomial of exponential degree, computes its dense square-free part, or writes a high-precision root in binary. It also does not apply to arbitrary short circuits for univariate polynomials: such a circuit need not describe a polynomial with few terms. We use the sparse isolation algorithms of [30, 31]. There are two adjustments to make before using their arithmetic bounds with succinct numbers. First, rounding a Newton center to a grid with exponentially many bits cannot be treated as an elementary operation. Second, the precision bound for nested gates must depend on the magnitudes of their coefficients, rather than the bit heights of previously constructed rational approximants. We give these adjustments explicitly. 

Sparse weak covers. After collecting equal exponents and deleting zero coefficients, divide out the lowest monomial. Differentiating and again dividing out the lowest monomial removes one term. The resulting fractional-derivative chain has length at most k. On the positive axis, the union of its root sets has fewer than k2 elements, by Descartes’ rule. Accordingly, among k2 + 1 distinct positive grid points, one is a nonroot of every member of this chain. It can be selected by exact sign comparisons; one may maximize the minimum absolute value of the chain polynomials. All newly selected endpoints below use this entire original chain. The weak-cover algorithm of [31, Algorithm 1] recursively retains small intervals covering derivative roots and refines the intervening sign-change intervals. It makes polynomially many refinement calls and returns polynomially many disjoint intervals of width at most 2 −L, covering all real roots in its domain. Some intervals may contain no root. For a polynomial with simple real roots separated by more than 2 −L+2 ,retaining exactly those intervals with opposite endpoint signs gives genuine isolating intervals. Root-free boundary sentinels are discarded. If the nonzero coefficient magnitudes lie in [2 −τ , 2τ ], the refinement bound is polynomial in k, log( n +1) , log( τ + L + 1), where n is the degree [31, Theorem 11]. Here τ is an archimedean magnitude bound, not the bit height of a rational representation. The fractional-derivative chain satisfies the same type of bound with T = τ + k⌈log 2(n + 1) ⌉. Short powers of two give positive endpoints below and above all its positive roots by the Cauchy bounds. Alternatively, dilation X = SY by a power of two above the upper root bound places the search in the source’s unit interval. Dilation preserves sparsity and increases the magnitude exponent by at most n log 2 S, which has short binary length. The negative roots are treated by replacing X with −X.

Removing the large floor operation. Let I = ( a, b ) contain exactly one simple real root, put 

w = b − a, and maintain N = 2 2ℓ

with ℓ ≥ 1. At the three quarter points xi = a + iw/ 4, evaluate

λ = xi − (xj − xi)vi

Otherwise perturb it by at most δ, using the nonroot grid just described. Accept the resulting interval I′ if its endpoint values have opposite signs. Directly from the clipping, snapping, and perturbation rules, 5r

Boundary tests use points at distance 3 w/ (4 N ) from either endpoint, perturbed by at most w/ (16 N ). They succeed when the root is within w/ (4 N ) of that endpoint. Otherwise split within w/ 16 of the midpoint. A successful accelerated step increments ℓ; a linear step replaces it by max(1 , ℓ − 1). (2(Γ + L) + 2 ).

Thus N always has a short repeated-squaring description. If a quarter point is exactly the root, return a small interval around it with nonroot endpoints, rather than a singleton later lost by sign filtering. These are the geometric invariants used in the sparse refinement analysis [30, Theorem 7]: accelerated width reduction, fixed-fraction endpoint movement, boundary success, and cluster success. The initial endpoint/Obreshkoff-lens argument takes O(log n + log( T + L)) steps. Thereafter the one-circle regions lose roots at most k − 1 times, and a chain with unchanged root set has that same length bound. Changing the endpoint-movement fraction from 1 /4 to 1 /8 only changes absolute constants. Each sparse evaluation uses O(k log( n + 1)) arithmetic operations. The resulting weak cover therefore uses polynomially many rational-SLP operations and PosSLP comparisons, without a large floor or endpoint bit expansion.

Rational approximation and root indices. Approximate the retained coefficients of p to error 2 −P

and apply the same differential operator to obtain a rational-SLP polynomial ˜q. It suffices to take 

P = H + n(H + E + A + 6) + ( k − 1) ⌈log 2(n + 1) ⌉ + 3 ⌈log 2(k + 1) ⌉ + 10 .

Each coefficient changes by at most k(n + 1) s2−P . On circles of radius ν = ϵη/ 16 about the roots of qϵ, its magnitude is at least 2 −H νn. Midpoints belonging to one original root have diameter less than σ/ 8, whereas distinct original-root clusters are separated by more than σ/ 2. Grouping consecutive midpoints at gap σ/ 4 recovers the distinct-root order and multiplicities. Any midpoint in the selected group approximates that root within 2 −B . The removed monomial contributes zero once to the distinct root list, with its binary multiplicity recorded separately. 

A shared precision schedule for nested gates. Let M be the input description length and r

its number of root gates. The product of their degrees is at most 2 poly( M ). Standard algebraic-height and norm bounds give a fixed H0 = 2 poly( M ) controlling all valid-prefix values, their nonzero conjugates, coefficients, roots, and nonzero root differences. Include the collected coefficient expressions and the final ordinary-arithmetic output in this bound. Enlarge it to control the conditioning of their rational arithmetic on neighborhoods of the true earlier roots. This bound concerns the input’s algebraic values, not the heights of later rational approximants. Exact zero coefficients and zero denominators can be recognized by approximating past this fixed nonzero gap. The preceding construction needs coefficient precision at most K(B + H0) to deliver root error 2 −B , for one common K = 2 poly( M ). Crucially, this dependence is linear in B. Choose 

Bi = Kr−i+1 (Bout + H0 + 1) , 1 ≤ i ≤ r, 

90 enlarging K once for coefficient conditioning. All these exponents have polynomial binary length. Compute each root approximant once and keep it in a shared arithmetic DAG. Each gate uses polynomially many operations in M and log( Bi + H0), because the weak-cover bound uses the controlled coefficient magnitudes and root separation. It does not use the bit heights of the cached numerators. There are consequently only polynomially many new arithmetic operations and PosSLP queries. Approxi-mating the final output past its semantic gap determines its sign, including zero. Invalid gates are detected when first encountered. This proves the theorem. The restriction remains substantive for real feasibility. In particular, the characteristic polynomial of the critical algebra need not have polynomially many terms. The theorem enlarges the root-certificate language without supplying such certificates for unrestricted ETR. 

# Q.4 Inverses of series/parallel rational circuits 

A second extension permits rational inverse operations. Consider a shared DAG with one variable X,nonnegative scalar leaves, positive scalar multiplication, and the operations 

P (F, G ) = F + G, S(F, G ) = F G F + G .

We call these parallel and series composition, respectively. Identically zero operands are eliminated using 

S(0 , G ) = 0 and P (0 , G ) = G, including S(0 , 0) = 0. All retained nodes are positive for X > 0. Values at zero are defined by continuity. An inverse gate specifies such a function F and a target b, and returns the unique finite r ≥ 0 with F (r) = b. Negative scalar leaves, a constant function, or an absent finite inverse make the gate invalid. 

Theorem 26. Sign and validity for polynomial-size arithmetic circuits with nested series/parallel inverse gates are in PPosSLP , and therefore reduce to PosSLP using the adaptive-query compiler. 

The degree of the reduced rational function may be exponential in the shared-DAG size. The convergence bound below has no degree parameter. The theorem concerns the stated positive grammar, rather than arbitrary rational circuits accompanied by an assertion of monotonicity. 

Concavity and duality. On the positive quadrant, H(a, b ) = ab/ (a + b) has positive first partials and Hessian Hess H(a, b ) = − 2(a + b)3

Composition therefore shows that every retained function is increasing and concave. Its derivative is strictly positive unless it is constant: an active variable occurrence contributes positively at every operation. There is also a useful second concavity. Define 

F ∗(t) = 1

F (1 /t ) .

Duality exchanges series and parallel composition, replaces a positive constant by its reciprocal, and replaces a positive scalar multiplier by its reciprocal. Thus F ∗ belongs to the same grammar and is concave. Differentiating twice, with x = 1 /t , gives

Endpoint validity. The finite value F (0) is computed recursively using the continuous series rule. For 

F (∞), use an infinity tag: parallel composition with infinity is infinite, while 

S(∞, a ) = a, S(∞, ∞) = ∞.

Zero nodes have already been eliminated. For a nonconstant function, the exact validity condition is 

F (0) ≤ b < F (∞).

Equality at the lower endpoint returns zero. Equality at a finite upper asymptote is invalid. Nonconstancy is detected by the active-variable tag, or equivalently by F (1) > F (0). These rules handle removable zero denominators such as the apparent 0 /0 in S(X, X ) at zero. 

Coefficient stability and nested gates. We spell out the precision bound to avoid a dependence on the heights of generated Newton endpoints. For total description length n, the cleared root equations have degree 2 poly( n). Algebraic height and norm bounds supply a fixed L = 2 poly( n) controlling exact gate values, finite endpoints, their nonzero differences, and F ′(r). Every listed nonzero value has magnitude between 2 −L

and 2 L.Fix the exact zero pattern. On the positive parameter box in which nonzero leaves and X lie in [2 −L−1, 2L+1 ], positive numerator/denominator clearing gives a uniform derivative bound 2 G, with G =2poly( n), for F (X) − b and finite endpoint tests. One positive denominator monomial supplies its lower bound. These estimates depend on the input grammar, not the rational cache. Suppose incoming scalar errors are at most e. For 0 < t ≤ r/ 2, (66) gives F ′(x) ≥ 2−L/4 on [ r − t, r + t]. Choose 

t = 8 e 2G+L, e ≤ 2−G−2L−4.

Then t ≤ r/ 2, and the two true endpoint residuals dominate their perturbations. The nearby positive grammar has a unique inverse ˜r in this interval, with |˜r − r| ≤ 8e 2G+L. Its Newton computation still obeys (67) exactly. The zero-root and endpoint-equality cases are handled by separated algebraic sign tests before this argument. Thus a local gate has error at most Ke + δ, where one may take K = 2 G+3 L+10 and enlarge it for ordinary arithmetic gates. For at most s gates, a common local precision δ = 2 −B with 

B = s(⌈log 2 K⌉ + 1) + G + 2 L + Bout + 30 makes the accumulated error smaller than both the stability threshold and 2 −Bout . All precision exponents have polynomial binary length. Each approximant is computed once in a shared cache, using polynomially many rational operations and PosSLP comparisons. Taking Bout ≥ L + 5 completes the sign and validity tests. 

92 An exponential reduced-degree example. Set F0 = X + 1 and 

Fj+1 = S(Fj , 1) + S(Fj , X ).

Only constantly many nodes are added per step. If Fj = P/Q is reduced, the next pair is 

Pnew = P ((1 + X)P + 2 XQ ), Qnew = ( P + Q)( P + XQ ).

These polynomials are coprime. Indeed, put A = P + Q, B = P + XQ , and C = B + XA . A common divisor of A, B would divide X − 1, but A(1) > 0. Also P (0) > 0, so P is coprime to A, B , and C is coprime to both 

A and B. Positive coefficients persist. The reduced degrees consequently satisfy deg Pj = 2 j+1 − 1, deg Qj = 2 j+1 − 2.

This verifies exponential degree without relying on unreduced formal expressions. No representation of the general ETR critical eliminant by this grammar is claimed. 

# Q.5 Monomial-weighted positive rational inverse gates 

Let P, Q be nonzero univariate polynomial circuits using only + and ×, the variable X, and nonnegative scalar leaves from earlier gates. Their support and degree may be exponential. For a binary integer D > deg Q, put 

F (x) = xD P (x)/Q (x) (x > 0) , F (0) = 0 .

The value at zero denotes the continuous extension, so it does not evaluate Q(0) −1 when Q(0) = 0. An inverse gate returns the unique nonnegative solution of F (x) = b for b ≥ 0, returning zero directly when b = 0. Negative scalar leaves or b, zero P or Q, and a failed degree condition are invalid. 

Proposition 18. Sign and validity for polynomial-size rational arithmetic circuits with polynomially many nested inverse gates of this form belong to PPosSLP , and hence admit the previously established many-one compilation to PosSLP . The gates fit the same shared scalar pool as the preceding root and inverse constructions. Proof. Write p = deg P and q = deg Q. 94 Q.6 Root gates with ordered coefficient blocks 

The sparse-root construction has an extension in which the number of nonzero coefficients may be exponential. What is supplied instead is an ordered decomposition into positive coefficient blocks. Write 

fj (X) = Xaj Pj (X), deg Pj ≤ bj − aj ,

0 ≤ aj ≤ bj , bj < a j+1 (j < s ),

where each nonzero Pj is given by a circuit with nonnegative scalar leaves and the operations addition and multiplication. The exponents are written in binary. Consider 

p(X) = 

> s

∑

> j=0

cj fj (X).

Zero cj are removed, and zero leaves are identified before the degree recurrences are used. The signs of the remaining cj are arbitrary. If all cj are zero, the selected-positive-root gate is invalid. After the sign tests, write cj = sgn( cj )|cj | and include |cj | among the shared positive scalar leaves for rational perturbation; its sign is held fixed. The number s + 1 of blocks may be polynomial in the input length. 

Theorem 27 (Ordered-block root gates) . Subject to the semantic-height and adaptive-compilation lemmas used above, sign and validity for polynomial-size arithmetic circuits with nested selected positive roots of the supplied ordered-block polynomials are in PPosSLP , and reduce to PosSLP . Either distinct-root indexing or indexing with multiplicities is allowed. Scalar coefficients are shared with the previously supported root and inverse gates. 

This is a theorem about the supplied representation. It does not provide a short ordered-block decompo-sition of an arbitrary arithmetic circuit. For example, the family

Proof of Theorem 27. Construct the true Rolle chain and work downward from Ls. There are O(s2) internal roots. Fix a semantic exponent H0 = 2 poly( N ) covering their magnitudes, nonzero critical-value gaps, and separations between distinct roots of all chain numerators; here N is the full input length. This uses the same algebraic degree and norm bounds as the earlier root-gate compiler. Uniform one-root and two-root estimates supply the complex separation and imaginary-part bounds. We do not adjoin exponentially many complex roots to the shared semantic field. Suppose the roots αi of Nk+1 have been constructed. Test Nk(αi) = 0 using the fixed semantic gap. If it is zero, carry the same root into the current list. Equation (68) increases its multiplicity by one. Every carried root is thus an alias of a simple root at a deeper stage; it is not obtained by splitting an approximate multiple root. Choose buffers of radius δ = 2 −H0−10 about sufficiently accurate approximants to the αi. They contain no other current-stage root. On each remaining interval, Lk is strictly monotone, so endpoint signs identify at most one new simple root. Strict dyadic lower and upper bounds close the two unbounded ends. One may take 2 −H0−2 = 256 δ and 2 H0+1 , and use fixed cluster disks of radius δ/ 4; a zero-root cluster then stays below the searched positive interval. A second exponent H1 = poly( n, H 0, s ) bounds the nonzero endpoint values and derivatives. This distinction matters: an endpoint evaluation can cost nH 0 bits in its product bound. The buffers remain fixed by H0; they are not recursively shrunk to demand a self-referential gap estimate. For a new simple target, use one fixed rational approximation of the original positive leaves. In Nk,the largest active suffix uniquely supplies the highest-degree term and the smallest active suffix the lowest nonzero order. Their coefficients remain nonzero under these perturbations, so degree and multiplicity at zero are unchanged. Rouché bounds preserve all root clusters in fixed small disks. Any split multiple-root clusters remain in the excluded buffers, and nonreal and negative clusters remain outside the positive isolators. The selected simple isolator contains exactly one perturbed root. The rational numerator still has the Wronskian structure. Binary search on dyadic exponents first gives a factor-two positive bracket using O(log( H0 + 2)) sign tests. A further polynomial number of midpoint bisections puts its entire one-circle disk inside the disk of Lemma 27. There are now at most s − k complex roots in that initial disk. One-circle disks of nested subintervals are nested, so their root set can change at most s − k times. Sagraloff’s refinement lemma [30, Lemma 6] bounds a consecutive portion with unchanged root set by O(log n + log(Γ + B + 2)) iterations. Here Γ controls the initial magnitude and 2 −B is the requested width. The floor-free secant, clipping, and boundary construction already proved above retains the required width and endpoint-movement bounds. 

97 It uses only ordinary circuit evaluations of the numerator and its derivative, and PosSLP sign tests. No coefficient variation is computed or bounded globally. If the rational refiner encounters an exact rational root, that is an approximation to its matched true internal root. The semantic label is retained; no later zero test assumes that the true algebraic-coefficient polynomial vanishes at that rational point. For a simple target, on a circle of radius 2 −B the local polynomial product has only one factor costing B

bits. All other factors use fixed separation bounds. The required earlier-scalar precision therefore has the form K(B + H1 + 1) with K = 2 poly( N ). Carried roots forward that request to their deeper simple source. Critical-value tests use their fixed semantic gaps. A reverse cache schedule with base 2 K absorbs the additive term. Each of the polynomially many internal roots is approximated once, and all later arithmetic shares its stored rational circuit. Degree products, height exponents, and precision encodings remain within the previously proved semantic bounds. Thus the number of operations and PosSLP comparisons is polynomial. The adaptive compiler gives the stated final reduction.

For the scalar fold family h = 7 , a = 1 , ∆ = 25, use only scale-one folds. After t ≥ 1 folds, with k = 2 t,

Three coordinates are necessary in this family. Since k is two or four modulo six, D ≡ 6 (mod 7) and

# Q.9 A shared pool of real root constants 

The certificate numerators can be enlarged to polynomial circuits with a polynomial-size shared pool of real scalar constants. The constants are defined by the supported binary-root and monotone inverse circuits of Theorems 23 and 24. Their entire defining DAG counts toward witness length. Root gates do not depend on the polynomial variables. We also permit the original disjoint-variable flattenable format: each numerator is one selected coefficient of a polynomial SLP, and only the fixed-degree norm or conic residual operations are applied before the perfect-pairing square. Fresh coefficient variables give at most constantly many copies of each generating circuit. Arbitrary-depth arithmetic on coefficient-extraction leaves is excluded. 

Proposition 20. Ideal membership for this format in the fixed-degree pure-monic critical algebra is in BPP PP .Polynomial-size norm or conic certificates in this format would therefore still imply ∃R ⊆ coMA PP .Proof. Let F be the residual and let L ⊂ R be the field generated by the shared scalar pool. The perfect residue pairing gives 

U = ∑

> α

λ(eαF )2, F = 0 in A ⊗ L ⇐⇒ U = 0 .

Perfection survives scalar extension. At the selected real embedding, all summands are nonnegative. The finite Laurent formula and the bounded coefficient extractions represent U as one selected coefficient of a short polynomial circuit using the same scalar pool. For a norm numerator, its two appearances in a square and the final residual square use at most four disjoint-variable copies. If the total description length is s, the exact pool has field degree and logarithmic heights at most 2 poly( s).The coefficient expression has singly exponential degree, coefficient bit height, and monomial count. The ordinary height inequalities therefore give a computable LU = 2 poly( s) such that 

U̸ = 0 =⇒ U ≥ 2−LU .

99 This bound concerns the exact semantic expression, before any approximation circuit is built. Formal differentiation and coefficient ℓ1-norm bounds likewise give G = 2 poly( s) such that the sum of absolute partial derivatives of this coefficient, on a box containing the scalar pool and its perturbations, is at most 2 G.Approximate every shared scalar to error at most 2 −LU −G−3 using rational SLPs. The root algorithms allow this binary precision with polynomially many operations and PosSLP queries; their uniform schedule is increased by the requested final accuracy. Sharing preserves polynomial circuit size. The resulting coefficient 

˜U satisfies 

| ˜U − U | ≤ 2−LU −3.

Clear scalar denominators by numerator/denominator pairs and compare ˜U with 2 −LU −1 using CoeffPosSLP .This distinguishes zero from nonzero. Validity of the scalar pool and nonzero scalar denominators is handled by its sign procedure. The BPP PP simulations of PosSLP and coefficient sign compose after amplification. Two-sided bounded error is sufficient for the stated MA verifier. This is an archimedean verification argument, not modular evaluation of algebraic root constants. It does not give short ordinary integer SLPs for their exact values or residues. The shared-pool restriction also matters: exponentially many independently generated radicals can have a double-exponential compositum degree, invalidating the polynomial-bit separation exponent used above. 

# Q.10 Exact conic certificates for dense convex compositions 

Let P (T ) be given by a + , × circuit with nonnegative scalar leaves. For each gate polynomial f , define DD f (U, V ) = f (U ) − f (V )

U − V , Bf (U, V ) = f (U ) − f (V ) − f ′(V )( U − V ).

These are polynomial identities, including at U = V . At a constant leaf both expressions are zero; at T they are one and zero. Addition is componentwise. For multiplication, DD f g = f (U ) DD g +g(V ) DD f ,

Bf g = g(V )Bf + f (V )Bg + ( U − V )2 DD f DD g .

The second identity follows by adding ( f (U ) − f (V ))( g(U ) − g(V )) to its first two terms. Memoizing 

f (U ), f (V ), f ′(V ), DD f , Bf at every original gate adds only constantly many gates per node. If U, V are SOS-conic, all the displayed factors other than the explicit square are SOS-conic by nonnegative-coefficient syntax. Thus BP (U, V ) has a short exact conic description, even for exponential degree and exponentially many expanded terms. 

Theorem 28. Let 

q(X) = ( X − u)T A(X − u) + d, h(X) = P (q(X)) + bT X + c,

F (r) = 2 rP ′(d + r2)

100 has a nonnegative-leaf circuit, obtained by automatic differentiation, and is nonconstant and strictly increasing on r > 0, with F (0) = 0. β = bT v > 0, γ = √β, r = F −1(γ), α = u − (r/γ )v.

where

makes h unbounded below, and b = 0 leaves a constant sign test. If A = 0, the image test likewise handles all nonzero b. No formula divides by γ = 0. Symmetry, PSD, rank, image membership, and the rational solves use polynomial-size linear algebra with PosSLP comparisons. PSD LDL elimination pivots on a positive diagonal; negative diagonals reject, and a residual block with zero diagonal must be identically zero. The sign of μ is decided by the root-circuit theorem. When μ > 0, one further constant τ = √μ gives 

h = τ 2 + C, with τ̸ = 0. Equivalently, h/τ 2 = 1 + C/τ 2 is the usual conic certificate. There are only polynomially many shared scalar gates, so Proposition 20 applies. Block sums add the minima and conic remainders; an invertible affine substitution preserves these identities. The theorem includes P (T ) = ∏ 

> j

(1 + aj T 2j

), whose expanded support may be exponential. It does not cover a general sum of overlapping quadratic compositions: their minimizer may require a coupled system. In particular the squared quadratic residuals of the unrestricted ETR critical objective do not satisfy the hypotheses. No rational-coefficient descent of the new root-constant certificates, and no improved unrestricted ETR containment, is asserted.

103 Proof. The lemma gives |F (z)| ≤ η/ 2. Since E(z) ≥ 1,

In particular, an approximate identity suffices even when the norm inequality is an equality. The index j may have polynomial bit length. A fixed polynomial-time generator supplies each Pj as one coefficient of a polynomial SLP, divided by one common scalar denominator whose nonvanishing is checked. Its size is polynomial, and its syntactic degree and coefficient bit-height bounds are singly exponential. Ordinary polynomial SLPs are included. Polynomially many supported real scalar root/inverse constants may be shared by all circuits. They are independent of X and j. The scalar η belongs to this same pool. Arbitrary arithmetic of polynomial depth on coefficient-extraction leaves is not included. Expanding U (F ) uses at most four copies of a Pj coefficient description per summand. All SOS and basis indices can be summed with polynomially many index bits and disjoint coefficient variables. Clearing the known scalar denominators by a positive square leaves one coefficient-sign comparison. The shared real-pool verifier first fixes semantic degree/height and absolute gradient bounds, then approximates the pool to the corresponding precision. It checks this comparison and η > 0 in BPP PP . Its error is two-sided. Guessing a short certificate gives the stated conditional coMA PP consequence. Dense completeness does not supply the missing size bound. If h ≥ δ > 0 at all real roots, choose a short η > 0 with 2 ηDB 2M ≤ δ. In the etale real algebra R ⊗ R ≃ Rr × Cq , the element h − ηE has a square root: it is positive in the real components, and every complex component has a square root. Its coefficient vector can be approximated by a rational vector so closely that even one dense rational polynomial 

P satisfies the norm inequality. No short description of that vector follows. For completeness, these dense coefficients have singly exponential bit heights. Every complex coordinate has modulus at most C, so every basis value is bounded by Ub = CM . If T is the real evaluation matrix, the nonzero integral discriminant gives 

| det T | ≥ 2−D/ 2. Cofactors imply ∥T −1∥2 ≤ I0 := D2D(DU b)D. For a short bound Amax ≥ | h(z) − ηE (z)|

at every complex root, the square-root coefficient vector is bounded entrywise by V := I0D(Amax + 1). Put 

B0 = C2M , bounding all multiplication structure constants. Rounding these coefficients with error ϵ ≤ 1 gives 

√U (h − P 2 − ηE ) ≤ D4B20 (2 V + 1) ϵ. 

Thus dyadic precision with singly exponential value suffices. Its exponent has polynomial bit length; the common denominator has a short scalar SLP, and the dense numerators have singly exponential bit heights. This supplies no coherent short generator. The positive slack plays the same role as the residual lower bound in the convex rational-center certificate. In that setting a short Newton computation and an explicit Bregman identity construct the certificate; such a constructor is still missing here. There is a useful distinction between numerical convergence and matrix representation. For a real symmetric nonsingular matrix with eigenvalue magnitudes in [ l, u ], the scaled step 

A 7 −→ A + luA −1

2√lu 

preserves signs and puts the magnitudes in [1 , (u + l)/(2 √lu )]. Its condition ratio is at most √u/l . Thus polynomially many scaled steps suffice even when the logarithm of the initial condition ratio is exponential. Ordinary Newton steps subsequently converge quadratically. The supported scalar root pool supplies the scales; it does not supply coherent descriptions of the matrix inverses or products. Indeed, if δ is the Jacobian, the trace matrices are H1 = HM δ and Hh = HM δh , with Mf denoting quotient multiplication. The short inverse of H does not provide a short inverse of Mδ or its scalar denominator. After the congruence, a weighted form is JM ′, while its inverse is M ′− 1J. Newton steps therefore involve conjugation by J, which need not preserve the commutative multiplication algebra. Ordinary matrix powers cannot be replaced by quotient powers. Nor does real-point convergence imply residue-norm convergence: in 

Q[x]/(x3 + x), the error 1 − (1 + T x )2 vanishes at the only real point for every T , while its residue-square norm grows as a nonzero degree-four polynomial in T . The outstanding step is a short coherent Gram approximation with the global error and known scalar scale required above.

Proposition 24. There is a polynomial-size real polynomial homotopy from 1 to a that is a unit at every complex point throughout 0 ≤ T ≤ 1 and is positive at every real point throughout that interval. Proof. Choose ℓ = ∑ ciXi with integer 0 ≤ ci ≤ D such that Im ℓ(z)̸ = 0 at each nonreal point. There are at most D/ 2 pairs, so a random choice succeeds with probability greater than 1 /2. Let Mℓ ≥ 1bound all |ℓ(z)|, and let ν ≤ 1 be a positive lower bound for every nonzero | Im a(z)|. ϵ > 0 satisfying ϵM ℓ ≤ ν/ 2, and set

Both endpoints are units. Continuation from the root 1 is thus well-defined and conjugacy compatible. The minimum modulus also has a singly exponential logarithmic bound: its square is a real quartic in T , with interior extrema at roots of its cubic derivative and the usual algebraic degree/height bounds.

# Q.14 A generic recursive norm lift is also obstructed 

The preceding fixed-formula argument does not allow the new square coordinates to depend on an old ternary point. A Brauer-group argument rules out that stronger generic construction for the h = 13 family. This is not a lower bound for step-specific arithmetic circuits. We use the following standard fact, with its short proof included. For a smooth projective quadric surface 

X over a characteristic-zero field K, a nonsquare discriminant implies that Br( K) −→ Br( K(X)) is injective . (71) Over a separable closure, the Picard group is generated by the two rulings. The nonsquare discriminant exchanges these rulings, so the invariant Picard group is generated by the hyperplane class (1 , 1), which already descends to X. The Hochschild–Serre exact sequence Pic( X) −→ Pic( X)Gal −→ Br( K) −→ Br( X)

106 therefore makes the last map injective. Smoothness also gives Br( X) ↪→ Br( K(X)). These two exact-sequence statements are recorded, for example, in Colliot-Thélène’s Arizona Winter School 2015 lectures [32], Sections 4.2 and 4.4, printed pages 19–20.

An eventual cycle of curve components would return to a geometrically integral component and give the forbidden map on its smooth projective normalization; infinitely many rational points force that geometric integrality. The conclusion does not exclude invariant surfaces, arithmetic branching, nonrational constant fields, or a different update formula at each step.

# Q.16 A limit on deterministic phase samples 

There is a simple obstacle to replacing the random magnitude test by one fixed short list of universal multipliers. Fix integers T > B + 2. Let K1, . . . , K q be arbitrary integers and let nonnegative weights wi

sum to one. Suppose ∑

> i

wi cos(2 πK iδ) ≤ 12 for every δ ∈ [4 /2T , 2−B ).

Then 2T −B−2 ≤ 6q , q ≥ T − B − 2log 2 6 . (72) To see this, consider the phase vectors (4 jK i/2T mod 1) qi=1 , for 0 ≤ j < 2T −B−2. Partition each coordinate circle into six half-open intervals of length 1 /6. If two vectors occupy the same box, their difference supplies an allowed nonzero δ whose every cosine is strictly greater than 1 /2, a contradiction. In our magnitude test B = 32, while T can have exponential value. Thus a universal deterministic sampler requires exponentially many support points. This does not rule out a sampler depending on the input SLP: the collision index in the proof need not have a short arithmetic description. Nor does it concern a random list that succeeds with high probability for each fixed displacement. It excludes only the proposed replacement by a fixed polynomial number of multipliers valid on the entire phase interval. 

# R Remaining obstacles and unsuccessful routes 

The real and integer arguments share a way of recovering signs, but they leave different arithmetic objects to be evaluated. For an SLP, a short modular value is already computable in polynomial time. For the real construction, even after the ordered-rank refinement, we have two nested products over formal branches. A product algorithm retrieves a count and then exponentiates by its value. We do not know how to absorb this nonlinear operation into the same counting function as the later sum. There is a restricted way to remove the coefficient layer. If the factors ci ∈ GR( pa, e ) of ∏

> i<D

(u + ci)occupy only polynomially many known residue classes modulo p, every specified coefficient is in FP #P. Write 

ci = λv(i) + pβ i, retrieve the class multiplicities Nv , put nv (S) = |{ i ∈ S : v(i) = v}| , and expand 

∏

> i

(u + ci) = ∑

> j<a

pj ∑

> |S|=j

(∏

> i∈S

βi

) ∏

> v

(u + λv )Nv −nv (S).

Each surviving subset is polynomially describable, and its remaining factor is a short SLP. Apply Lemma 14 inside the raw subset count, after retrieving the multiplicities. If this residue-class condition held at every node, it would give another way to remove the high-derivative layer. General Fourier nodes do not preserve the condition. The ordered-rank proof in Section J.5 reaches C4P without it. More generally, the same expansion works when the reference product ∏

> i

(u + λi) has a short SLP and the λi are individually computable: divide out the polynomially many deleted factors by a truncated reciprocal at infinity. This includes some families with exponentially many residue classes, but no such description is established for the general homotopy factors. 

111 This identifies a concrete route to a stronger result. Suppose the integer family Zh in Section J.5, uniformly over its random seeds, had GapP PH representatives modulo every short integer. Lemma 16 would decide each sign in BPP PP ; random isolation and repetition would then give ∃R ⊆ BPP PP ⊆ C2P. The first-event reconstruction would also give a containment in PH PP after simulating the outer random choices by relativized polynomial-hierarchy quantifiers. A reduction of those integers to coefficients of polynomial-size SLPs would also suffice. The present norm construction gives neither representation. This is a separate problem from reducing a constant in a counting-level ledger. For integer SLPs, the remaining distinction is between sampling a phase and averaging it exactly. Each sampled phase involves nonlinear postprocessing of a retrieved counting value. Putting that postprocessing inside another exact count would raise the oracle level. The present argument therefore gives BPP PP , and does not derandomize it to PPP or replace it by a randomized reduction to a single PP predicate. There is a related source-side question. The original short-conjunction argument only proves that some root can be isolated by some list. It does not count roots, and different lists may select the same root or overlapping subsets. The small decision bound therefore does not automatically give the same bound for counting real solutions or connected components. The ordered-rank refinement labels every real root, and Theorem 6 now supplies the additional signed-Morse experiment that gives total point counting in FP C4P.This does not by itself count connected components at the same level. The accompanying supplementary material includes the earlier general resultant construction, unsuccessful attempts to remove these remaining operations, and finite exact checks. The checks cover the rational filter, nonreal common zeros, small-characteristic homotopies, Galois-ring coefficient identities, and signed noncanonical beta representatives. Additional checks cover the small-bias generator, its random-access algorithm, the signed phase sums, and the scale search under arbitrary labels in its transition interval. They test the individual identities and boundary cases; the uniform complexity conclusions depend on the arguments in the paper.
